**Why one vertex with inline legs.** The entry's identity covers every leg, so (a) partial visibility is impossible by construction — there is no interleaving in which a reader sees three of four legs; (b) `entry_digest` is a tamper-evident identity for the whole accounting fact, which is what a bundle proves and what an external ID should bind to; (c) the balance law is checkable from the vertex alone; (d) immutability is a property of one object rather than of a cluster. The `Leg` vertices and `HAS_LEG`/`POSTS_TO`/`FOR` edges are **derived projections written in the same substrate transaction** by Folio (the only writer, through the secure view), so GQL pattern queries and indexes work over legs without the projection ever becoming authoritative; a reference oracle (§16.2) asserts projection-equals-payload after every commit, and recovery rebuilds projections from payloads. `recorded_at` is **not** a payload field: it is the commit stream's assignment, read as the creating vertex's `created_at: CommitSeq`, linked to its creating `MarkerRef`, which is precisely why it cannot be forged (constraint 6).

Posting entries require their registered positive-leg arity; control entries have no legs. `Resolution(Posts)` uses posting arity; `Resolution(Voids|Expires)` is the release-only control described in §5.4. Multi-currency entries balance per unit (FA-INV-01 clause `per-unit`) and, when `base_totals` is present, in the book's functional currency under the named FX law with a valuation residual absorbed by the designated leg's `base_amount` and recorded in `base_totals.residual` (clauses `base`, `base-residual`, §5.2). This does not add a native-unit posting leg. Native-amount residuals appear as explicit law-named posting legs only when that separate law requires them. A `Memo`/`OffBalance` account's legs balance among themselves in their unit and never against monetary legs; a `Statistical` account admits **single-sided** legs when its unit's `balance_policy = SingleSided` (headcount, square footage — Oracle's STAT journals do not balance, by design), excluded from the balance law, from monetary registers, and from statement footing unless a definition names them (clause `memo`).

**Storage form (normative).** The substrate stores scalar properties only (`Int(i64)`, `Decimal`, `Text`, `Timestamp`, `Bytes`, `Bool`, `Null`; no structured values). An `Entry` vertex therefore carries exactly: `_fa_payload: Bytes` — the canonical `fa:entry:v1` encoding of every field above, bounded by the `max_entry_bytes` registry row and hard-bounded by the substrate's 64 MiB scalar — plus scalar **mirrors** for predicates and indexes: `_fa_digest: Bytes[32]`, `book_id: Bytes[16]`, `class: Int`, `effective_day: Int` (DateDay) only when `effective_date = Some(day)` (omit it for None), `idempotency_key: Bytes`; `recorded_at` is the vertex's `created_at: CommitSeq`. `legs`, `unit_totals`, `evidence`, `tags`, and `supersession` live only inside the payload. Derived `Leg` vertices mirror `side: Int`, `account_ref`, `unit_id: Int`, `effective_day: Int`, and `amount: Decimal` when `|mantissa| · 10^(18 − scale) ≤ 10^34 − 1` (a per-unit registry check; otherwise `amount_bytes: Bytes` and `amount: Null`). GQL predicates and sums run over mirrors; FA-INV-16 compares the decoded payload to every mirror after each commit, and recovery rebuilds mirrors from payloads.

### 4.3 Accounts and the chart

The chart is a forest of `Account` vertices under `CHILD_OF`, versioned in valid time: an account is `Leaf` or `Summary`, and only leaves accept legs; promoting a leaf to summary (because children were added) is a journaled schema effect that is legal only when the leaf has no legs effective after the promotion date, else the kernel refuses with the exact entries that block it. `type` and `normal_balance` are fixed at creation (a change is a new account plus a reclassification plan). `unit_policy` fixes whether an account holds one unit (most GL accounts), any currency (an FX clearing account), or any unit (an inventory or securities account). `dimension_requirements` lists the dimensions every leg must carry (cost center on every expense leg, for example); the kernel rejects a leg missing a required dimension with the list of allowed values. `control_of` ties the account to a `Subledger` whose detail must reconcile (FA-INV-12). `balance_limit` names an escrow domain (§5.5) such as "may not go negative" or "may not exceed budget". Chart templates (a US GAAP small-business chart, a bank chart aligned to call-report lines, an IFRS chart) are data under `templates/` and are imported as ordinary journaled schema effects.

### 4.4 Units and amounts

`Amount { unit: UnitId, mantissa: i128 }`, interpreted as `mantissa × 10^-scale(unit)`. The unit registry fixes the scale; the mantissa is always at that scale (no per-amount exponent), so equality, ordering, hashing, and summation are integer operations. `Amount` values of one unit form a **bounded** ordered abelian group: the group laws hold for every representable value, every sum is a left fold in canonical order with checked operations, any intermediate overflow is a refusal (never a wrap, never a silent saturation), and the proof lanes state a representability hypothesis — the reference domain for the Lean lanes and for `fa-reference` is unbounded integers, and `fa-reference` carries its own independent limb arithmetic rather than `fgdb-bigint`, so a shared arithmetic bug cannot make the differential green. **A `UnitId` is immutable, scale included.** A precision change creates a *new unit version* (`USD@2` → `USD@4`) with a registered exact `ScaleConversion` law (× 10^k, lossless by construction) and a `Redenomination` control record; historical payloads are never rewritten or reinterpreted — a mantissa of 100 means 1.00 under `USD@2` forever — and folds across versions apply the law at read time, recording it in the certificate. An economic redenomination (a new currency replacing an old one) is a different unit with a conversion *rate* document, never a scale change. **Legs carry a side and a non-negative mantissa** (`mantissa > 0` for monetary legs; zero only on registered memo legs): a negative amount is refused with the side-swapped form suggested, so every posting has exactly one representation, per-side limits are meaningful, and `i128::MIN` is unreachable; a reversal is a side swap with the same mantissa, never a signed storno. Register accumulators (`debits_posted` and the rest) are non-negative monotone sums bounded by the registry's `max_register_mantissa`; a post that would overflow a register is refused. The full algebra is §6.

### 4.5 Parties, instruments, entities, and ownership

`LegalEntity`, `Party`, and `Instrument` form the "who and what" of REA. Ownership (`OWNS {pct}`), control (`CONTROLS {basis}`), and relatedness (`RELATED_TO {basis}`) are valid-time edges, so "related parties as of the transaction date" is a temporal closure query, not a spreadsheet. Beneficial ownership, officers, and family relationships use the same edge kinds with a `basis` vocabulary registered in `relationship_bases.toml`. Every bank account is an `Instrument` `HELD_AT` a `Party` of kind `Bank` and `MIRRORS` a GL account; statements for it are documents whose parsed `BankLine`s match legs (§9.5).

### 4.6 Documents, events, rules, principals, approvals, attestations

The evidence graph records *why*. A `Document` is a content-addressed object (§9.1). An `Event` is the REA economic event (an invoice was issued, a payment was received) that a Canon rule turns into one or more entries; the event and the entry are both retained with `DERIVED_FROM`/`GENERATED_BY` lineage so the derivation itself is testable (re-run rule version *v* over event *e* and compare digests). A `Principal` is a human, a service, or an agent (`program`, `model`, `session_id`, parent session lineage) — agent attribution is as first-class as human attribution. An `Approval` is a journaled object binding a principal, a capability, a recorded sequence, and the **proposal digest** of what was approved — never the committed object's own digest, which would be a fixed point. The identity graph is acyclic by construction (normative order in Appendix A): an `EntryProposal` (every entry field except `plan_certificate_oid`, `approvals`, `proposal_digest`, and `entry_digest`) is hashed to `proposal_digest`; the dry-run plan certificate covers the plan including `proposal_digest`; each `Approval` binds `proposal_digest` together with the book, target coordinate, security-policy epoch, and the complete effect set (`effects_digest`); the committed `Entry` envelope adds the plan certificate reference and the ordered approval transcript, hashed by Appendix A's explicit recipe to `entry_digest`; the accumulator leaf, findings, and report certificates hash outputs that already exist. Changing one leg after approval changes `proposal_digest`, so the approval no longer binds and the commit is refused (FA-INV-10 clause `approval-binds-proposal`); adding the first valid approval requires no fixed point; and promotion that replays materially different effects produces a different `proposal_digest` and therefore demands re-approval. Attestations and checkpoints sign transcripts that explicitly exclude their own `signature`/`signed_digest`/`signatures` fields (Appendix A). An `Attestation` is a signed statement about an object (§9.4).

Equal proposal digests are necessary but not sufficient for approval reuse. Commit also validates the complete ordered effects, intended target coordinate, bound security-policy epoch, and current authority and segregation-of-duties requirements. A fresh certificate may describe unchanged proposal bytes without silently authorizing a changed branch, coordinate, policy, or execution principal.

**Applicability is distinct from identity.** Reuse checks the proposal digest, complete effects commitment, intended book and target coordinate, security-policy epoch, current SoD, and execution authority. Equal legs or equal proposal digests alone are insufficient. Draft promotion names its intended trunk target in the approved semantic proposal; execution transport is not part of semantic identity. Historical validity of an approval survives later revocation as evidence; it does not grant a scheduler fresh execution authority. Scheduled reversals bind the target proposal, rule/date/amount policy, and approving authority without a reference cycle, and recheck current execution policy when emitted. Acceptance vectors change each bound field independently.

The policy registry must choose whether an epoch change requires reapproval or re-evaluation of historical approvals under the current policy. It cannot decide that from equal proposal bytes alone. The chosen current-time source/type, strict or inclusive expiry comparison, and charged daily epoch are recorded at the specified admission/publication point.

### 4.7 Time attributes on an entry

| Attribute | Who sets it | Axis | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `effective_date` | The intent or rule (user-supplied, validated) | Valid time | Some(day) for period-scoped entries; None for book-scoped controls; payload applicability and extract selection follow §7.1 |
| `recorded_at` (= vertex `created_at: CommitSeq`, linked to its creating `MarkerRef`) | The commit stream — **never user-supplied** | System time | When the books learned of the entry; drives "as known at" and all evidentiary claims |
| `document_date` | The source document | Attribute | The date on the invoice/statement; drives cutoff tests |
| `approved_at` (per approval) | The commit stream, at approval commit | System time | SoD evidence |
| `migrated_from.source_recorded_at` | Migration provenance | Attribute | What the legacy system claimed; never promoted to `recorded_at` |

Effective dates are civil dates (`DateDay`) in the book's calendar; the posting kernel does not accept instants for `effective_date` (a time zone is a policy of the book, not a property of a leg). Recorded membership is the creating CommitSeq. UTC information enters via §7.1a's TimeEvidence with its declared precision; HLC-bearing markers are a future source, never assumed live.

### 4.8 Books, branches, and coordinates

Every read and write names a **coordinate**: `BookCoordinate { book, branch, known_at: SystemSelector, effective: EffectiveSelector }` where `SystemSelector = Head | AsOfSeq(CommitSeq) | AsOfTime(Instant)` and `EffectiveSelector = All | Period(period_ref) | Range[start,end) | AsOf(DateDay) | YearToDate(DateDay) | InceptionToDate(DateDay)` (the last two serve fund, grant, and project accounting). The default is `trunk@head, All`. Branches (drafts, scenarios, consolidation runs, restatement rehearsals) are substrate branches when available and intent-log drafts otherwise (§15.2); in both cases the coordinate vocabulary is identical, which is what makes the interim posture honest rather than a fork in the API.

### 4.9 Identity and stable handles

Every object an agent might cite has a stable handle: `EntryRef { vid, entry_digest }` (the digest makes the handle self-verifying), `AccountRef { book, code }`, `PeriodRef { calendar, code }`, `DocumentRef { oid }`, `FindingRef { vid, digest }`, `CertificateRef { oid }`, `BundleRef { oid }`. **Identities are derived, never allocated.** Because the substrate has no key constraints yet (Appendix H R-CON), uniqueness is enforced by identity: `vid(Entry) = H("fa:vid:entry:v1" ‖ book_id ‖ principal_scope ‖ idempotency_key)`, `vid(ExternalId) = H("fa:vid:extid:v1" ‖ book_id ‖ system ‖ id)`, `vid(Account) = H(book_id ‖ code)`, `vid(Period) = H(calendar_id ‖ code)`, `vid(Unit) = H(book_id ‖ unit_code)`. The substrate's `create_vertex` refuses `AlreadyLive`/`IdentitySpent`, so a duplicate idempotency key or a duplicate legacy journal number is a point conflict on one vertex — no label scan, no serialization point — and a retry reads the key-derived `IntentOutcome` with one point read (§5.9). Target-derived reversal/replacement/resolution identities enforce separate target-uniqueness laws, not the book/principal/key outcome namespace. `EntryRef { vid, entry_digest }` is identical across machines only for the same scoped identity and canonical committed envelope, including its certificate and approval transcript. Proposal identity is separate (§4.6); the envelope digest makes the handle self-verifying. When the substrate's key constraints land they become a second, redundant guard, never a replacement for derived identity (FA-INV-09).

### 4.10 Schema posture

`Entry`, `Leg`, `Account`, `Period`, `Unit`, `Register`, `Attestation`, `Approval`, and every control object are **Strict**; `Party`, `Document`, `Finding` are **Shaped** with a bounded overflow map; nothing is **Open**. Schema objects are versioned in the commit stream like data, so a chart change is time-travelable and branchable and a prepared report invalidates precisely when its schema epoch moves.

### 4.11 Reserved system surface

The substrate exposes `vid`, `labels`, `created_at: CommitSeq`, and `retired_at` today (its `_valid_from`/`_valid_to` arrive with valid-time contracts, Appendix H R-VT; there is no `_commit_time` or `_provenance` system property — provenance is `principal_ref`/`session_ref` in the payload plus `PREPARED_BY` edges). FrankenAccounting's own system properties are prefixed `_fa_`: `_fa_payload`, `_fa_digest`, `_fa_graph`, `_fa_effective_day` (on elements that carry an effective date), `_fa_fold_digest` (on registers), `_fa_certificate` (on report rows), `_fa_redaction` (on documents under a redaction contract). System edge types `DERIVED_FROM` and `SAME_AS` are the substrate's; FrankenAccounting reserves `SUPERSEDES`, `RESOLVES`, `GENERATED_BY`, `EVIDENCED_BY`, `ATTESTS`, `CITES`, and `READ`.

---

## 5. Folio: The Posting Kernel (Bet A1)

Folio is the only writer of the ledger graph. Every path that creates accounting state — an agent's typed intent, a Canon rule firing on an event, a bulk migration, a close run, a restatement case, a two-phase resolution, an expiry sweep — enters Folio as a **posting proposal** and leaves as either a committed entry (with its derived projections) or a typed refusal naming exactly what to change. There is no second door.

### 5.1 The posting pipeline

```text
Intent | Event+Rule | Batch | Control            (§10, §5.8, §7.5, §8.3)
        │
        ▼
 1. ADMIT     capability check (books/entities/accounts/periods/ops/amount ceiling) — before any observation (FA-INV-10)
 2. RESOLVE   accounts by code→VId at effective_date; dimensions; units; period(s) via Almanac; open items for `settles`
 3. CANON     if rule-driven: evaluate rule_version over event under stripped Cx → candidate legs (§10)
 4. ABACUS    exact totals per unit; base conversion under FX law; law-named native allocation legs and recorded base-valuation residual; allocation conservation (§6)
 5. LAWS      balance law (per unit, per entity, base); leaf-only; unit policy; required dimensions; entry-class
              admissibility for the resolved period state (Appendix C); supersession target validity; pending/
              resolution rules; SoD (preparer ≠ approver; dual control where the matrix demands); idempotency key
              lookup; POST-EVALUATION AUTHORIZATION of every generated leg (account, dimensions, amount) against
              the caller's current caveats ∩ the rule's RuleAuthority grant — step 1 admitted only the verb and
              the explicit inputs, because a rule's effects do not exist until step 3
 6. PLAN      build the dry-run plan: resulting legs, register deltas at head, period decision, SoD decision,
              escrow rights required, evidence closure heads, certificate of the plan (this is what `--dry-run` returns)
 7. COMMIT    one bounded substrate transaction: create Entry vertex (payload + mirrors) + derived Leg vertices/edges +
              supersession/resolution/evidence/approval edges + ExternalId vertices + limit-cell CAS + DraftSet/Case
              membership; point-read witnesses on Period state, Account effectivity, open items; identity-derived
              vids make duplicate keys a point conflict (§4.9)
 8. ACK       return EntryRef{vid, entry_digest}, MarkerRef, the plan certificate, and next_actions
 9. DERIVE    (asynchronous, supervised) register maintainer advances watermark; indexes; findings; subscriptions
```

Steps 1–6 are pure given a pinned snapshot; the dry run *is* steps 1–6 with no step 7, and its certificate names the snapshot, so a plan an agent reviewed is exactly the plan that commits if the snapshot's witnesses and applicable aggregate-basis guard (§5.10) still hold (the substrate's commit-time witness validation — first-committer-wins over the point reads the plan recorded — makes "the period closed between my dry run and my commit" a typed conflict, not a silent post into a closed period).

### 5.2 The balance law

For every committed entry and every unit `u` appearing in its legs: `Σ amount(leg) over debit legs in u = Σ amount(leg) over credit legs in u`, exactly, in `u`'s scale (FA-INV-01 `per-unit`), and, in a multi-entity book, per `(u, legal_entity)` (`per-entity`). When the book requires functional-currency equivalents (every book does for monetary units other than its base), each monetary leg carries a **valuation** `base_amount` computed under the entry's `fx_law_oid` from the rate documents named in `base_totals.rate_doc_oids`, and `Σ base debits = Σ base credits` exactly (`base` clause). The rounding residual between the two base sums is **not a leg**: it is absorbed into the valuation of one designated converted leg — `base_totals.residual { leg_ordinal, amount }`, chosen by the FX law's deterministic rule (the converted leg with the largest base amount on the deficient side; ties by lowest original leg ordinal) — so no unit's per-unit balance is touched and the permitted residual is derived from the selected rounding law's bound and the exact pre-rounding base-balance equation (`base-residual`). Base-unit legs retain their at-par valuations and never absorb a residual; if the deficient side has no converted leg, refuse with the law named. For independently rounded half-family conversions of an exactly balanced rational base vector, `|residual| ≤ Σ ½ ulp_i`; directed unit-rounding laws use `|residual| < Σ ulp_i`, and coarser quanta use their registered bounds. An exact pre-rounding difference is an economic/valuation difference requiring declared FX treatment, never a rounding residual. Unsupported laws and invalid adjusted valuations are refused. The counterexample that fixes the rule: EUR legs `Dr 0.01, Dr 0.01 / Cr 0.02` at a rate of ½ under `HalfUp` value to `Dr 0.01, Dr 0.01 / Cr 0.01` in USD; the credit leg's valuation becomes `0.02` with `residual {ordinal 2, +0.01}` recorded, EUR still balances `0.02 = 0.02`, USD valuations balance `0.02 = 0.02`, and no USD leg exists. Period-end revaluation (§6.4, §12.4) never edits a valuation: it posts an ordinary functional-currency entry between the revalued account's registered `Valuation` contra leaf and the unrealized-FX account, balanced per unit trivially; historical-cost release on settlement is the application rule reversing the accumulated valuation adjustment for the settled item as part of its realized-FX computation. Memo and statistical units balance among themselves (`memo` clause). There is no tolerance. **Cross-unit settlement** — a USD receipt applied to a EUR invoice, the most common multi-currency entry — does not balance per unit and is expressed only through the chart's registered `AnyCurrency` **conversion account** (`fx_conversion_account`, the role hledger's `equity:conversion` plays): the application rule family plans one pair of legs per unit on that account so every unit balances, plus a `RealizedFx` leg under the entry's FX law for the difference between the open item's historical `base_amount` (net of released valuation adjustments) and the settlement's base amount, **paired with an equal and opposite functional-currency leg on the conversion account** so the functional currency also balances per unit (clause `realized-fx`): `Dr Cash USD 1,100 / Cr Conversion USD 1,100; Dr Conversion EUR 1,000 / Cr AR EUR 1,000; Dr Conversion USD 50 / Cr RealizedFxGain USD 50` when the invoice was valued at USD 1,050 — every unit balances, the conversion account's functional balance is zero, and the gain is a real leg. Those legs appear in the dry-run plan and are never inserted silently; the conversion account's functional-currency balance must be zero after each period-end revaluation (`fa verify fx-conversion`), and `ApplyPayment`/`ApplyReceipt` carry typed `differences` (discount, short-pay, write-off, bank charge, realized FX — Appendix B) so no difference between cash and the open amount ever becomes a plug. A proposal that is unbalanced is refused with the per-unit imbalance and, when a registered rounding account exists and the imbalance is within the unit's minor unit, the *suggested* residual leg — suggested in the error, never applied silently.

In the advertised multi-entity balancing scope, base debit and credit totals must also equal separately per legal entity. Select a residual within the entity with the rounding difference; derive its entity from the designated leg's authenticated dimensions. Opposite entity differences cannot cancel at book level. The residual Amount uses the base unit, with signed mantissa denoting the adjustment, not a separate posting. The current singular residual Option admits only representable cases: every entity must pass its base equality, and proposals requiring additional residual adjustments are refused as unsupported. A bounded versioned residual list, at most one adjustment per affected entity, is the selected deferred extension; its schema, codec, checker, fold and cost rows must be active before admission. No duplicate entity field is required when leg dimensions supply it.

The EUR AR relief and its paired EUR conversion leg in this example use the same carried USD 1,050 basis. Valuing the conversion leg independently at the current USD 1,100 spot amount would make the stated six-leg entry unbalanced. A same-unit foreign-currency settlement at a changed rate likewise declares its carrying-value and cash valuation policy; a conversion route or a registered historical-cash policy must be explicit in the plan. A supplied `base_amount` is a checked assertion of the declared basis, never permission to type a balancing value.

### 5.3 Registers are folds

A native-unit register does not determine its functional-currency carrying amount. Every statement or procedure that consumes functional amounts names a registered **functional fold** over the visible legs' `base_amount` values, with identity valuation for legs already in the book's base unit. Its definition fixes canonical contribution encoding, base unit, coordinate, dimension grouping, class/disposition treatment, watermark plus tail, and checkpoint/rebuild behavior. FA-INV-04 applies to these numerical measures as well as to native measures. The contribution digest commits the valuation fields and basis references actually used. Co-located base measures and a separate derived functional register are equivalent storage choices; neither changes the authoritative journal or puts register writes on the posting path.

Functional folds preserve the per-entity base equality required by §5.2; their contribution encoding includes the selected leg's entity and any recorded residual adjustment.

A `Register` is the derived cell `(book, branch, account, unit, period, dimension_subset_key) → {debits_posted, credits_posted, debits_pending, credits_pending, entry_count, watermark, fold_digest}`. `RegisterDefinition`s (versioned data) declare which dimension subsets are materialized per account family — "cash by legal entity", "expenses by cost center and project" — exactly as a data warehouse declares aggregates, except the definition is journaled and the cells are *certified folds*.

**Materialization doctrine.** The posting transaction writes the entry and its projections only; it never touches a register (rejected alternative (b) in §3.6). Registers are maintained by the **register maintainer**, a supervised asupersync region that consumes the book's committed delta stream in commit order and advances a published `watermark: CommitSeq` per register definition. A balance read at the head is `register(cell) ⊕ fold(legs of cell in (watermark, head])` — the substrate's "watermark + tail" exactness pattern — so reads are O(1) at the watermark plus a bounded tail scan, and the register is never stale in the answer, only in the cache. A balance read `AS KNOWN AT s` with `s ≤ watermark` folds forward from the nearest retained register checkpoint; registers keep periodic `RegisterCheckpoint`s (a journaled, content-addressed snapshot of all cells at a `CommitSeq`) so historical folds are bounded. `fold_digest` is the BLAKE3 of the canonical leg contributions sorted by `(commit_seq, entry_vid, ordinal)`, so it is also a commitment to commit order (it matches the accumulator's leaf order, §13.2), two registers that claim the same value from different histories cannot collide, and a recomputation can be compared by digest. In 1.0 persisted registers exist only for `trunk`; draft and scenario registers are computed in the scratch book (§15.2) and never persisted to trunk, while the key keeps `branch_id` so no schema change is needed when substrate branches land.

**Verification by recomputation** is a first-class operation (`fa verify registers`): fold every cell from the journal at the register's watermark and compare digests; a mismatch is a correctness defect (FA-INV-04), and the register is discarded and rebuilt from the journal. Registers are never authoritative; statements are computed from registers but *certified* against the fold.

**Interim posture (Appendix H row R-REG).** Until Ripple's `CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW … REFRESH INCREMENTAL` is live, the register maintainer is FrankenAccounting's own actor over `Database::delta_since` (live today: a pull iterator over a pinned snapshot returning `LogicalDeltaBatch { coordinate_entries, commit_seq, commit_marker_identity }` strictly after a cursor, gap-free, in commit order, with typed refusals `BeyondFrontier` and `DeltaCursorRetired`). Its contract: call `ensure_delta_window(&commit_cx, watermark)` after every open (a checkpoint-opened book has an empty window below the frontier until then); consume in commit order; apply idempotently keyed by `(commit_seq, entry_vid, cell)`; decode legs from the `CreateVertex` payload bytes, never from `Leg` mirrors; publish the watermark only after the cells it covers are durable; checkpoint at a cadence no longer than the retention floor (`checkpoint_cadence ≤ retention_floor` is an `slo.toml` row under FA-CFG-01), so that on `DeltaCursorRetired` it discards cells and rebuilds from the last `RegisterCheckpoint`; and run as a supervised region woken by `fad` after each commit, because the feed is pull-only. It is the same contract a Ripple circuit will satisfy, so the switch is a replacement of the maintainer's body, not of any reader. The maintainer's correctness is proved against the reference oracle (§16.2) under the lab runtime with crash points between delta consumption and watermark publication.

### 5.4 Two-phase entries

TigerBeetle's two-phase transfer is adopted as the two-phase **entry**: an entry of class `Pending` reserves its amounts in the `*_pending` register fields without posting; it is later resolved by exactly one entry carrying `pending.resolution_of = EntryRef` and a `RESOLVES {disposition}` edge: `Posts { source_ordinals }`, whose posted sub-vector is `Entry.legs`, posts a **per-leg balanced sub-vector** of the pending legs (same account, unit, side, and dimensions per leg; each posted mantissa ≤ its pending mantissa; balanced per unit) and releases the remainder leg by leg, `Voids` releases everything, `Expires` is the system's void at timeout. A scalar `Posts { amount }` is accepted only as sugar for a two-leg single-unit hold; for N-leg or multi-unit holds the resolution either names the sub-vector explicitly or names a registered proportional law (`ProportionalPost { law, rate }`): the registered law must produce a balanced sub-vector satisfying each native `(unit, legal_entity)` equality and original-leg capacity bound. One candidate law, for exact rate in [0,1], computes one law-rounded target from each such partition's common pending debit/credit total, then allocates that same target independently over debit legs and credit legs by largest remainder; ties use the lowest original leg ordinal. This construction is conditional on the law's rate and rounding domain, not the uniquely necessary algorithm. The law defines omission of zero-rounded monetary legs with source-ordinal mapping; an empty result or invalid posting arity is a typed refusal naming `VoidReserve` when release is intended. No zero-amount posting or silent disposition change is invented. The resulting entry still passes every other registered balancing dimension, base, account and lifecycle law; per-side native conservation alone does not certify those predicates, so the hold `Dr A 60, Dr B 40 / Cr Cash 100` posted "for 60" is unambiguous: `{Dr A 60 / Cr Cash 60}` and `{Dr A 20, Dr B 40 / Cr Cash 60}` are different sub-vectors and the caller must say which. Resolution is unique by identity: `vid(Resolution) = H("fa:vid:resolution:v1" ‖ pending_vid)`, so a manual void racing the sweep is an `AlreadyLive` point conflict, never a double release. A pending entry may carry `timeout_ns: u64` measured from the latest TimeEvidence tick in a strictly preceding commit (§7.1a), derived from its journal position, never from an unrecorded wall clock; expiry is a visibility predicate plus a deterministic sweep (the substrate's `ExpiryEpochAdvance` pattern): the `fa` daemon orders one `ExpirySweep` control entry per configured tick carrying rollback-resistant time evidence, which emits `Expires` resolutions for every pending entry that is due at that tick (`observed_utc_ns ≥ deadline`, as defined below) — so expiry is journaled, replayable, and never "best effort". Resolution is a new object; the pending entry is immutable; resolving twice is a typed refusal citing the prior resolution (`pending_already_posted|voided|expired`). Holds, authorizations, escrow deposits, grant encumbrances, and purchase commitments are the accounting uses; the pending/posted split is visible in every register and in the trial balance as a declared column. Pending register cells are keyed on the **pending entry's** period and unit; every resolution adjusts the `*_pending` fields of *that* cell regardless of the resolution's own effective date, while a `Posts` resolution's posted legs land in the resolution's own period — so a March hold expired in April releases March's reservation rather than leaving it reserved forever. The sweep runs as the `System` principal with an intrinsic `post_closed` for `Expires` only; `Expires` and `Voids` are admissible in every period state except `Future` (Appendix C), so a hold never becomes unreleasable because its period closed. **Reserved availability is defined by equations, not by a head read.** For a limited account with `net = debits_posted − credits_posted` in debit-normal sign and reservations `pending_out` (pending legs that would move value out) and `pending_in`: a proposed outflow `x` is admissible iff `net − pending_out − x ≥ lower_bound`, and a proposed inflow against an upper bound iff `net + pending_in + x ≤ upper_bound` — pending outflows reserve, pending inflows never finance, posted flows are final; so with cash 100 a pending outflow of 80 blocks a second 80 and an unrelated posted 30, exactly TigerBeetle's `debits_pending + debits_posted + amount > credits_posted` rule generalized to N legs. The sweep processes due holds in `commit_seq` order of the pending entries, at most `max_batch_entries` per transaction, continuing in further transactions under the same tick's evidence with a journaled `SweepCursor` control record (last processed pending vid), so a backlog larger than one batch neither stalls nor violates the batch bound; a due-but-unswept hold is still `pending` in every register and is reported as `due` by `fa triage` (`FA-CFG-02`). Scheduled reversals use the same cursor discipline.

A `Resolution(Posts)` has its positive posting sub-vector only in `Entry.legs`, with `source_ordinals` mapping each leg to its original pending leg. A `Resolution(Voids|Expires)` has empty legs and `control = ReservationRelease`; its original pending amounts are obtained from the immutable `pending.resolution_of` target. `Resolution` is disposition-sensitive for arity, rather than uniformly a posting or control class. Other control classes remain empty-legged.

Every resolution removes the **full original pending amount** from each original pending cell exactly once. For `Posts`, that decrement consists of the consumed posted part plus the unused remainder released; only the selected sub-vector increments posted measures in the resolution's own period. For `Voids` and `Expires`, the full decrement creates no posted amount. A hold of 80 posted for 60 therefore changes pending by −80 and posted by +60, not pending by −20. Limit/reservation cells use the same conserved effects. This clarifies the existing intended release law; it is not evidence that the frozen plan committed the erroneous −20 operation.

A timed Pending's anchor is the latest TimeEvidence tick in a strictly preceding committed prefix. The anchor is derived from the Pending entry's committed position and retained journal evidence, not stored inside its proposal. A timed Pending without an admissible preceding TimeEvidence tick is refused; checked deadline-addition overflow is also refused. Initially a tick and a timed Pending entry cannot share a commit; required ticks are separate preceding control publications. Deadline is checked addition of the derived tick's instant and timeout; due means certified tick >= deadline. A manual Posts resolution is admissible only while the committed prefix contains no certified TimeEvidence tick with `observed_utc_ns ≥ deadline`: planning refuses a hold already due, and publication atomically rechecks under existing writer ownership that no such tick has committed, including since the plan's basis; otherwise it refuses under the registered refusal contract. Acceptance includes a Posts planned after a committed due tick, a due tick committed between plan and publication, and equality at the deadline. VoidReserve and Expires retain the existing unique-resolution race. The full hold stays reserved until a resolution commits. This is committed-tick expiry, not exact physical expiry. Any physical-duration estimate is conditional on the stated tick/source accuracy and sweep scheduling assumptions; downtime or unbounded backlog supplies no finite expiry-resolution bound. Capability expiry separately uses its declared current-time policy.

Both Pending schema renderings use `timeout_ns`, `resolution_of` and `disposition`. No stored proposal anchor is required; the committed journal position derives it, avoiding certificate/approval churn on intervening ticks. Replay retains the necessary tick closure.

### 5.5 Balance limits and escrow

An account's `balance_limit` names a FrankenGraphDB **escrow domain** (substrate §4.7) whose value is the account's net balance in its unit and whose bounds encode the limit: `credits_must_not_exceed_debits` ≡ lower bound 0 on a debit-normal account; a budget cap ≡ upper bound on an expense account's period cell; a loan facility ≡ upper bound on drawn principal. Posting consumes escrow *rights* reserved at step 6 and consumed at step 7, so a limit is enforced without locking the hot register: concurrent posts to a cash account proceed in parallel until rights are depleted, at which point the kernel escalates to the ordered path exactly as the substrate specifies — depletion is `NeedsRights`, never a silent overdraft. TigerBeetle's `balancing_debit/credit` flag ("transfer up to the limit") is adopted as the `Balancing` leg option: the leg's amount is `min(requested, available rights)` and the recorded amount is what moved.

**Interim posture (Appendix H row R-ESC; §0.4 decision 7).** Until escrow domains land, each account with a `balance_limit` owns one `LimitCell` vertex `{reserved_debits, reserved_credits: Decimal, generation: Int}` updated inside the posting transaction by a point read followed by `compare_and_set_vertex_property(generation, Some(g), g + 1, AbortWrite)`. It is a *reservation* cell, not a register (FA-INV-04 is untouched; `fa verify registers` also folds it), and it is the limited-account registered, scoped exception to Appendix G's "no hot object on the write path" law — limited accounts only, retired when escrow lands. A head-balance read over `Leg` vertices was rejected for this role: it would require a label-wide scan witness, which under the substrate's witness model makes every post in the book conflict with every other post in flight. Because the embedded posture has a single writer, contention is reported as the abort rate of interleaved write transactions, not as lock wait, and the §17 row is derived accordingly.

### 5.6 Subledgers and control accounts

A `Subledger` groups the detail accounts (every customer's receivable) under a control account. FA-INV-12 is a *synchronous* constraint: at every committed coordinate, the fold of subledger detail legs equals the control account's register. The kernel enforces it by construction for FrankenAccounting-native subledgers (a leg on a detail account *is* a leg on the control account's register cell by definition of the register key — the control account is the summary of its subledger subtree) and by matched-pair validation for externally fed subledgers (an external AR system posts a summary entry that must name the detail batch digest it summarizes; a batch whose detail does not fold to the summary is refused). Native subledgers are **one control leaf with a required `party` dimension plus the open-item graph** — never an account per customer; FA-INV-12's non-trivial form is the external-feed form. Only leaves own register cells; a `Summary` account's balance is the fold over its leaf subtree, materialized when a `RegisterDefinition` declares a rollup cell keyed by the summary's ref with `rollup: true` — never a second posting; an externally fed subledger's *detail* is not in the book at all (it is an attested document whose digest the summary entry names), so there is no second posting universe to double count.

### 5.7 Application, matching, and reconciliation

Each open item has both `open_native` and `open_base`, derived from its original valuation, valuation-adjustment postings and exact application/undo contributions at the selected coordinate. The application law names the carrying-value basis used for relief. One admissible law is `RemainingCarryingValue`: for a nonclosing application of native amount `x`, relief is the law-rounded exact ratio `open_base × x / open_native` at that prefix; the application that reduces `open_native` to zero relieves **all remaining** `open_base`. The other admissible basis, original carrying value plus a final remainder, produces a different per-application FX schedule and therefore requires a distinct law ID. The selected law is recorded; implicit switching between the two is forbidden. Reversing an application restores exactly the native and base relief that application recorded, rather than recomputing a proportion at the reversal's date.

Application (a receipt applied to an invoice; a credit memo applied to a bill) is the `settles` list on a leg, validated at step 5 against the target leg's open amount, creating `SETTLES {amount}` edges; over-application is refused with the open amount. **Application effects are versioned with lineage**: the open amount of a leg is the bitemporal fold `original − Σ SETTLES + Σ UNSETTLES`, where application undo is a deterministic projection of the reversal's authenticated target and netting law: the kernel emits the exact opposite of the target's native and base application effects, including when the permitted target is itself a reversal; `UNSETTLES` is a derived edge, not an independently entered payload so reversing a receipt reopens the invoice, and a `Replacement` must declare its own `settles` — application is **re-declared, never copied**, so it can never be applied twice. Target-side and unit validation (a receipt settles a debit-side open item in the same unit) and the net over-application check run over that fold. Bank reconciliation is matching `BankLine`s (parsed from statement documents, §9.5) to legs with `MATCHES {method, confidence}` edges: exact matches (amount, date window, reference) are deterministic; heuristic matches are *proposals* on a decision card that a principal with the `reconcile` capability confirms, so the confirmed match carries its approver; an unmatched line after the statement's close date is a finding. Reconciliation state is derived (`Instrument.reconciled_through: DateDay` is a fold over confirmed matches) and attested by a balance assertion (§9.4) that the statement's closing balance equals the mirrored account's register at that date.

### 5.8 Batches, bulk, and migration

A **batch** is a list of proposals committed atomically (TigerBeetle's linked chain): all commit or none, in order, each visible to the next (an invoice and its application in one batch). Batches are bounded (`max_batch_entries`, registry row) and one substrate transaction. **Bulk load** (opening a book from a legacy GL, a 50-million-line migration) uses the substrate's bulk protocol (§7.5 there): chunked, gap-free, source-row-digested manifests, staged and finalized through an ordinary transaction, with `Migration`-class entries carrying `migrated_from` provenance and the opening balances posted as an `Opening`-period entry whose evidence is the legacy trial balance document. A migration never forges `recorded_at` (constraint 6); the first fact the new book knows is "on this date we imported this history", which is exactly what an auditor wants to see.

The complete effect set has a canonical manifest whose `execution_order` lists `ProposalRef { vid, proposal_digest }` members in execution order, together with the intended book/target coordinate and any case or schedule authorization being exercised. `effects_digest` commits this ordered manifest; sorting the members as an unordered set is insufficient. A member's approval binds both its proposal digest and this manifest. Commit checks exact membership, order, current authority and SoD for every member; an approval of a case body alone does not approve an unspecified future set of entries.

References between co-approved members use the earlier member's immutable `ProposalRef`, not its final `EntryRef`: the final envelope includes approvals that themselves bind the set. The stored reference remains a `ProposalRef` after commit. A resolver may return the corresponding `EntryRef` only after checking both the stored vid and proposal digest against the committed object; it never rewrites the approved payload. Forward or cyclic member references are refused. Existing committed targets may continue to use `EntryRef`.

The manifest's authorization references name existing immutable case or schedule bodies, never a later transition, final Entry envelope or the manifest itself. The certificate and approvals retain the manifest preimage. Other same-batch source fields use EntryTarget only where their catalog permits an earlier member; no blanket weakening of existing-target constraints is implied. This manifest is a selected design option; its full protocol and canonical codec require independent construction/closure evidence before activation.

### 5.9 Idempotency and outcomes

Every proposal carries an `idempotency_key` (client-chosen, ≤ 64 bytes, scoped to the book and principal). A retry with the same key and byte-identical canonical semantic request (the Appendix B identity recipe, excluding transport-only dry_run/confirm_plan controls) returns the recorded outcome (`EntryRef` or the original refusal); the same key with different canonical semantic request bytes is refused as `IdempotencyConflict` with the digest of what was recorded (FA-INV-09, inheriting FG-INV-01). Outcomes are durable before acknowledgement; a client that times out and retries is the normal case, not an edge case.

Every admitted intent uses `vid(IntentOutcome) = H("fa:vid:intent-outcome:v1" ‖ book_id ‖ principal_scope ‖ idempotency_key)`. The immutable outcome binds the canonical intent digest and either its ordered committed EntryRefs or its durable refusal. Creating a successful outcome is atomic with all its entries, including target-derived reversal, replacement and resolution entries. Distinct intent classes cannot reuse one scoped key merely because their Entry vids differ. The retry path validates the request digest before returning the recorded outcome; a failed competing transaction never overwrites a winner. Recording a refusal is a separate authorized publication when no posting transaction commits, and its cost is explicit. Authorization still precedes the outcome lookup and any disclosure.

The refusal registry distinguishes admitted durable refusals from unauthenticated, unopened-book or environmental failures that cannot be published by this path. It must not claim a durable original refusal for those cases without an authorized recording mechanism.

### 5.10 The write path as substrate choreography

Folio holds the book's `Database` (a single-writer handle, `&mut self`) and the writer lease inside the `fad` daemon (or the embedding process). Each commit is one bounded substrate transaction: `db.begin(&txn_cx)` pins basis `b`; the reads at steps 2 and 5 are `WriteTxn::vertex_property` / `vertex_has_label` **point reads** — each a retained witness — on the `Period` vertex's `state`, the `Account`'s effectivity and posting flags, the `DimensionValue`s, the target `Entry` of a supersession or resolution, the open-item cell, and the identity-derived `vid`s of §4.9 (never a `vertices()` or label scan, which would widen the witness to the whole table and make every post conflict with every other); staging is `WriteTxn::write_ordered(db, batches)` with one `WriteBatch` per relation (the substrate's batches are single-relation) and the `Entry`/`Leg`/`ExternalId` vertex creates in the leading prefix; if staging returns `SnapshotAdvanced` because the frontier moved, `refresh_snapshot(db, &txn_cx)` revalidates every witness and the plan either continues or fails `PlanInvalidated` with a fresh plan; `WriteTxn::commit(db, &commit_cx)` validates the witnesses by first-committer-wins against the complete intervening history and publishes one capsule and one marker (two fsyncs). A single-leg-pair post with no supersession may instead be a plain `prepare_write`/`commit_prepared` batch whose period and account guards are no-op `compare_and_set_vertex_property(vid, key, Some(v), v, AbortWrite)` rows, which the substrate retains as dependencies; both forms are one publication. The dry run is the same transaction finished as `ReadClosed`. Covered point-dependency races—including period-state transitions, applications that write their witnessed open-item cells and duplicate key identities—produce typed conflicts (`exit 6`) with retry hints. Fold-derived mutations additionally require the aggregate guard below. There is no group commit on the embedded handle today (Appendix H R-GC), so Folio batches at the intent level (`PostBatch`, draft promotion) rather than pretending the substrate amortizes barriers it does not. The operation-cost registry (Appendix G) maps a single-entry post and a batch to one publication each today and to the substrate's fused autocommit-write arm when that protocol lands; every §17 posting gate is derived from those rows.

**Aggregate dependencies.** The intent registry's per-intent `dependency_class` column classifies dependencies: point-witnessed families keep their existing path; mutating plans whose effect amounts depend on a fold not fully covered by those witnesses record the target book/branch prefix and selector. The initial construction requires `current_head == aggregate_basis_seq` under the existing exclusive `&mut Database` immediately before publication, with no rebase policy; mismatch refuses PlanInvalidated and requires a fresh plan. This adds no lock and does not retain writer ownership across fold computation. Its atomicity is source-supported but UNRUN: a pinned-foundation conformance test must establish that no internal path publishes between comparison and the marker. Scratch-book sequences are never target bases; promotion replans on trunk and later members fold earlier staged effects in execution order. Required independent publications—including TimeEvidence, authorization evidence, maintenance or prior close-task commits—precede basis acquisition; any later publication invalidates it. Approvals may be obtained against an earlier plan or rehearsal, then the committing plan acquires a fresh basis after those publications. Reuse is permitted only when the complete canonical proposal, ordered effects, intended coordinate and policy applicability still satisfy §4.6, including current SoD and execution authority; an unchanged proposal digest alone is insufficient, and changed bindings require fresh approval. Any required plan-delivery AccessEvent also precedes the committing basis; a later delivery publication invalidates it. Each close task obtains a new basis. Per-intent policy bounds retries and either returns a typed conflict or requires an admissible soft-close/quiescent workflow; continuous ticks or maintenance cannot cause silent unbounded replanning. Acceptance covers both publication orders, approval publication between plan and commit, approval reuse after a fresh basis with each applicability binding checked, plan-delivery AccessEvents for writing principals, cache-plus-tail at the exact basis, retroactive legs, refresh, scratch-to-target replay, prior task commits, steady maintenance, and visibility-only changes such as MigrationRun Finalize. A Leg-label witness alone misses that last dependency. A narrower complete guard can replace head equality only under equivalent tests. Price invalidations in Appendix G; ordinary point-only posts acquire neither scan witnesses nor shared hot objects.

---

## 6. Abacus: Exact Money Algebra (Bet A5, part 1)

### 6.1 Representation and arithmetic

`Amount { unit, mantissa: i128 }` at the unit's registered scale (§4.4). Addition, subtraction, negation, comparison, and summation are checked `i128` operations; a unit mismatch is a type error at the API boundary (`Amount` arithmetic is only defined between equal units; conversion is always explicit under a law). Multiplication by a rational factor and conversion use **arbitrary-precision exact intermediates** from `fsym-bigint`, with common decimal scales cancelled first. The quotient, denominator and remainder remain wide through all nine rounding laws; only the final rounded magnitude is narrowed, then its sign is restored so `i128::MIN` remains representable. Allocation uses `fa-money`'s bounded `wide::U256` implementation to sum weights and divide exact products. Results are rounded under a named law back to the target scale — never a floating-point step or an intermediate at the wrong scale. `fgdb-bigint` is the independent test oracle for conversion; both it and FrankenSymPy check the separate U256 implementation.

`CanonicalDecimal` (scale 18, add/sub/neg only) serves the bounded substrate interchange domain. `Rate` stores a private reduced nonnegative `fsym-rational::BigRational` with a positive denominator; decimal and integer-ratio inputs preserve all admitted digits, including coefficients beyond 128 bits. Multiplication and division cross-cancel exact factors and return typed errors for zero divisors, exhausted budgets or cancellation. A rate document (§6.4) retains its source digits; a format limited to scale 18 must refuse values it cannot represent instead of silently narrowing a Rate. Percentages, basis points and per-mille use declared denominators. Nothing in `fa-money` imports a float.

**FrankenSymPy integration (`fa-pri`, within `fa-p7r`).** Parsing, composition, conversion and rate codecs require a caller-owned `ArithmeticBudget`, backed by `fsym-budget` and a borrowed `Cx`. It checks cancellation without reading a clock and charges deterministic work, cumulative buffer bytes and allocations; source/frame length is admitted before coefficient construction. It calls fallible metered APIs, never ordinary unchecked rational division. The caller carries inputs and budget policy into its computation certificate; these primitives do not themselves issue a certificate. Tests retain the 11,520-case conversion corpus and extend it with 1,728 cases using coefficients beyond 128 bits, all nine laws, signs, scale extremes and final representability boundaries, plus malformed codecs and resource refusals. The reference engine depends on registry data and calendar primitives only; the dependency gate rejects any path from it to production arithmetic. Foundation pins and actual wrapper-path allowlists change together. Journaled unit evolution and complete FX-law posting remain open in `fa-p7r`; this arithmetic integration does not close them.

### 6.2 Rounding laws

`rounding_laws.toml` registers every admissible rounding: `HalfUp`, `HalfDown`, `HalfEven` (banker's), `HalfAwayFromZero`, `HalfTowardZero`, `Ceiling`, `Floor`, `TowardZero`, `AwayFromZero`, and *regime* laws such as `SwissFiveRappen` (cash rounding to 0.05) and `TaxAuthority(jurisdiction, rule)` (a jurisdiction's statutory rounding for VAT lines). Each law row carries its exact definition over `(quotient, remainder, denominator)`, golden vectors at every scale, and its proof-lane row (the Lean lane proves `|rounded − exact| ≤ ½ ulp` for the half-* family and `< 1 ulp` for the directed family). Every computed amount in an entry, report, or finding records the law under which it was rounded; a report certificate lists the laws used; a change of a unit's default law is a journaled registry change that does not rewrite history.

### 6.3 Allocation laws

Allocation distributes an amount across *n* targets by weights and must conserve the total exactly. `allocation_laws.toml` registers `LargestRemainder` (Hamilton: floor each share, distribute the residual units to the largest fractional remainders in deterministic tie order), `ProRataResidualToNamed(target)` (round each share under a rounding law, post the residual to the named target leg), `Waterfall(tiers)` (fill tiers in order with caps), `Tiered(brackets)` (progressive bands, for tax and commission schedules), `EqualSplitResidualFirst`, and `ByUnitsThenValue` (two-key allocations). Each law carries the conservation theorem `Σ shares = total` (Lean lane), determinism of tie-breaking (CGSE policy id), and golden vectors; Canon rules invoke allocations by law id, and the resulting legs record the law and basis digest so an auditor can recompute a cost-center allocation from its driver table.

### 6.4 FX laws and rate documents

Each FX/application law identifies which legs use a rate-document valuation and which relieve or mirror a carrying value. Where the existing settled-leg reference and rule inputs determine the basis uniquely, retain that derivation. Where they do not—particularly a conversion-pair leg or imported legacy valuation—the canonical proposal must carry the missing basis reference and amount provenance. Register this field before admitting that form; do not infer the basis from the amount that happens to balance. Imported carrying values retain their migration evidence grade.

The FX law explicitly declares the conversion account's treatment for each supported entry class and revaluation procedure. The invoice-routing fixture must not count its technical balancing foreign-unit leg as a second economic exposure. This fixture does not establish a universal exclusion for every conversion-account position. The existing `fa verify fx-conversion` predicate remains scoped to its declared period-end policy. A broader exclusion or per-entry neutrality rule requires passing settlement, reclassification, close, reversal and revaluation fixtures; it is not established by the single six-leg example.

A rate is evidence, not configuration: an `FxRateDocument` is a `Document` of kind `Rate` with `{source, base_unit, quote_unit, rate: CanonicalDecimal, digits, as_of: DateDay|Instant, retrieved_at, retrieval_proof?}`. An `FxLaw` row fixes: which rate document class a book accepts for which purpose (`transaction`, `period_end`, `average`), the inverse convention (whether `EUR/USD` may be inverted and at what precision), the triangulation rule through the base unit when no direct rate exists, the rounding law for base-amount computation, the deterministic base-valuation residual assignment and its error bound, and any residual account used by a separately declared native allocation or difference-posting law. The base residual is absorbed under §5.2; naming an account does not authorize an extra native leg. Transaction conversion at step 4 records `fx_law_oid` and the rate document oids on the entry; period-end **revaluation** (monetary balances remeasured at the closing rate) and **translation** (a foreign-functional-currency entity's statements translated into the reporting currency with the cumulative translation adjustment in equity — ASC 830 / IAS 21 mechanics) are Canon rules under laws of kind `Revaluation` and `Translation` whose entries are `Revaluation`-class and carry the rate documents and the computation basis (§12.4).

### 6.5 Quantities beyond currency

Shares, units of inventory, hours, tokens, and commodities are units with their own scale; a securities or inventory account holds `AnyUnit` legs. A **`Lot`** is a derived open item `(acquiring leg, open quantity, open cost)` exactly as an invoice is an open item: relief creates `RELIEVES {quantity, cost}` edges, over-relief is refused with the open quantity, and `lot_laws.toml` registers `FIFO`, `LIFO` (admitted in US tax books only; refused by the IFRS registry per IAS 2.25), `SpecificId`, and `WeightedAverage` (with its exact rational and rounding law). Lot relief runs in Canon as `fold over open_lots(…) ordered by law`, whose bound is the input's length — total by construction without a static constant (§10.3). Unit conversion (ounces to grams, hours to days) is never implicit; a `ConversionLaw` row with exact factors exists or the conversion is refused.

### 6.6 Canonical encoding and cross-crate determinism

Amounts encode as `(unit_id: u32, mantissa: i128 LE)`. Rate format v1 is `FAR` followed by byte `1`, numerator and denominator byte lengths as two `u32 LE` fields, then minimal unsigned little-endian magnitudes. Zero has an empty numerator and denominator `1`; denominators are nonzero, coefficients coprime. Unknown versions, trailing/truncated bytes, high zero magnitude bytes and unreduced fractions are refused. Encoder and decoder share the caller's frame-size policy; decoding charges rounded coefficient storage before materialization. Laws use registry id and epoch. Arithmetic values and canonical bytes have no float or hash-iteration dependence. Cross-target budget equivalence and the full determinism harness remain verification work: a native test pass does not establish them. The target harness runs identical allocation/FX vectors on each supported target and compares digests.

### 6.7 Formal anchors

Lean lanes: group laws for `Amount`; rounding error bounds per law; allocation conservation per law; FX round-trip bounds (`convert(convert(a, L), L⁻¹)` differs from `a` by at most the registered bound under the inverse convention); balance-law preservation under reversal and replacement (a reversal's legs are the exact negation; a replacement chain's net equals the replacement). Each theorem has a proof-lane manifest naming scope and what remains unproved (bigint limb limits are assumptions, not theorems).

---

## 7. Almanac: Time, Periods, and the Close (Bet A2, part 1)

### 7.1 Two axes and a third coordinate

**Known-at** (system time) is the commit stream's `CommitSeq`: immutable, total, assigned by the substrate. **Effective** (valid time) is the civil-date axis period-scoped entries are anchored to and every period, account, dimension value, policy, and relationship is sliced on. **Branch** is the third coordinate. They are orthogonal: `FOR PERIOD FY2024-Q4 AS KNOWN AT 2025-03-03` answers "what did the books say on March 3rd about Q4", `… AS KNOWN AT HEAD` answers "what do they say now", and the difference between the two is the set of entries recorded after March 3rd with effective dates in Q4 — a restatement, an adjustment, or a late posting, each distinguishable by class. Omitted known-at means head; omitted effective means all time; neither ever means an ambient wall clock.

**Scope-aware control visibility.** The known-at prefix bounds the historical control facts available to interpret a coordinate: a Book-scoped control cannot contribute before its creating CommitSeq. Resolve its state from the committed control history in that prefix using the registered class/discriminant applicability rule, including any typed payload interval and lineage; do not invent a posting date or period. This historical selection is separate from the current-authority checks of §14.2. All monetary postings remain Period-scoped and satisfy period law.

Control-state/evidence closure and extract rows are separate selections. An All-time extract may list known Book controls when its declared kind/class scope includes them. A posting date/period filter does not by itself select an undated control as a posting row; an explicitly requested control extract uses its registered class-specific selection rule. A report still retains every admissible control/metadata dependency needed to interpret its selected postings, even if that control is not an output row. Neither absence from those rows nor `effective_date = None` removes required evidence, and no rule automatically includes every control in every extract.

Each supported control row must register its state-applicability and extract-selection rules alongside scope, authority and SoD; the registry/visibility checker must distinguish unsupported rows from completed rules. A missing rule or checker leaves that behavior unsupported and refused. A gate needing it cannot pass merely because an Option field or enum arm exists.

### 7.1a Time evidence: how instants enter the book

The substrate's markers carry no wall-clock instant today (`commit_hlc` is populated with the sequence number; Appendix H R-HLC), and even when they do, an accounting system must not depend on one process's clock for evidentiary claims. So instants enter the book only as **`TimeEvidence` control entries**: `fad` journals one per configured tick, and one immediately before every close, checkpoint, sweep, and statement issuance, carrying `{observed_utc_ns, source: DaemonClock | AuthorityDocument(oid) | SubstrateMarker, monotonic_ok}` under the `Cx` time capability, refusing a tick that is not strictly increasing. `AS KNOWN AT <instant>` resolves at the observed frontier to the greatest `CommitSeq` whose latest TimeEvidence tick at or before that sequence has `observed_utc_ns ≤ instant`; a tick entry is its own latest tick for this comparison; the certificate records the resolved seq and the bracketing ticks, and the robot envelope always shows the seq beside any instant. The strongest outsider-verifiable timestamp claim for an entry is therefore "recorded no later than the first witnessed checkpoint containing it", optionally strengthened by a registered `ExternalAnchor` (an RFC 3161 timestamp token over a checkpoint root). When the substrate adds an HLC field to `CommitMarker`, it becomes the primary source and the ticks remain as corroboration (§0.4 decision 6).

**Resolution contract.** Instant selection is a tick-bucket interpretation, not proof of an exact physical commit instant. Resolution records the book frontier used, lower tick, optional upper tick, resolved sequence, and precision/source grade. The upper bracket is explicitly open until a later tick exists. Repeating an instant selector against a later frontier may resolve differently; replay uses the saved sequence and closure. Golden vectors include ticks `(seq10,time100)` and `(seq13,time110)`: instant105 at heads11,12,13 resolves11,12,12. A stronger instant-history guarantee requires stronger evidence and a prospective policy change; it is not retroactively inferred from sparse ticks.

### 7.2 Fiscal calendars

A `FiscalCalendar` generates `Period`s deterministically from its kind: `Monthly12(year_start_month)`, `Weeks445|454|544(year_start_rule)`, `Week5253(end_rule: {LastWeekdayOfMonth(wd, m), WeekdayNearestEnd(wd, m)})`, and `Custom(explicit period list)`. Generation is a pure function with golden vectors through 2100 (including 53-week years and leap days). A book has exactly one calendar; a legal entity may declare a different statutory calendar used by its statutory book (§12.2). Adjustment periods (a 13th period for audit adjustments; an `Opening`/`Closing` period) are ordinary `Period` vertices with `kind` set. `Normal` periods ending on or before the book's `opening_date` are generated with `kind: Historical`, born `Locked` (by the generation rule, with no transition entry), excluded from `AS ORIGINALLY REPORTED` and from JET lookbacks by default; they admit only `Migration` entries and never transition (§8.6, Appendix C "Period kinds"). Special periods (`Opening`, `Adjustment`, `Closing`) ending on or before the `opening_date` keep their kind, are born `Locked`, admit only `Migration` entries and never transition (`[[pre_opening]]` in `admissibility.toml`), so a legacy year-end close is imported into its own `Closing` period, never re-dated. Civil-date arithmetic (proleptic Gregorian, day-of-week, month-end, ISO weeks, business-day calendars with holiday tables as data) is built in `fa-time` with no external crate, checked against a brute-force reference and the substrate's `Date` canonical type.

### 7.3 The period state machine and the period law

`state ∈ {Future, Open, SoftClosed, Closed, Locked}`. Transitions are control entries (class `PeriodControl`) with approvals: `Open` (from `Future`), `SoftClose`, `Close`, `Lock`, `Reopen(to: Open|SoftClosed, case_ref)`. Every transition is journaled with its principal and reason, so "who reopened March and why" is a query. The period it transitions dates the control entry, never the caller: an `Open` is dated on the period's first day, and every later transition (soft close, close, lock, reopen) on its last day, so the control always resolves to the period it names. The **period law** (FA-INV-03): a period-scoped entry's required `effective_date` resolves to exactly one `Normal` `Period` of the book's calendar, and an entry whose date lies in a range shared with an `Adjustment`, `Closing`, `Opening`, or `Historical` period must name its target explicitly in `effective_period` (a discriminator carried on the entry and on every intent; only `Adjusting`, `Closing`, `Elimination`, `Revision`, `Restatement`, and `Migration` classes may target a special period, and a `Normal` target is the default) so December 31 in period 12, adjustment period 13, and the closing period are three different periods with one civil date; the entry's class must be admissible for that period's state under Appendix C's matrix *and* the principal's capability must carry the required operation; `Locked` admits only case-bound restatement classes, case-bound `Closing` recloses, and reservation releases (`Voids`/`Expires`); `Closed` admits `Adjusting`, `Reclassification`, `Reversal`, and `Elimination` under the `post_closed` capability; `SoftClosed` admits everything `Open` admits except `Original` from non-controller principals (the soft close is "the accountants are closing; agents stop posting ordinary entries"). The read of `Period.state` at step 2 is a point-read witness (`WriteTxn::vertex_property`) validated by the substrate's first-committer-wins certifier at commit; a `PeriodControl` entry that commits between the plan's basis and the post's commit conflicts on that vertex, the post is refused, Folio re-reads the state, and the caller receives `PeriodStateChanged {was, now, closing_entry}` (`exit 6`). The plan certificate records the basis `CommitSeq` and the witness set, so "the plan that commits is the plan that was reviewed" is a checkable predicate, not a property of an isolation level. FA-INV-03 clause `nominal-zero` additionally requires that after the latest `Closing` entry of a fiscal year the nominal accounts' balances for that year fold to zero (§7.5).

This period-resolution law applies to entries whose registered scope is `Period`, including all monetary postings. They require an effective date and the applicable normal/special-period discriminator. A book-scoped control has no posting effective date or posting period; any policy applicability interval remains its own typed payload field. Its control-class authority and SoD law still apply at commit. Installing the first calendar and period definitions is book-scoped, so genesis neither fabricates a period nor exempts a monetary entry from period law. The scalar `effective_day` mirror is absent for book-scoped controls, matching `LeafEnvelope.effective_day: None`.

### 7.4 Selectors

Native selectors in FrankenAccounting's GQL extension and in every CLI/MCP read: `AS KNOWN AT <instant|seq|HEAD>`, `FOR PERIOD <code>[..<code>]`, `EFFECTIVE AT <date>` / `EFFECTIVE BETWEEN <date> AND <date>`, `AT BRANCH <name>`, and the two restatement views `AS ORIGINALLY REPORTED` and `AS CURRENTLY STATED` (head). `AS ORIGINALLY REPORTED` pins known-at to the closure of the period's first **`StatementIssuance`** (§12.5) — the journaled fact that statements left the entity — and, absent any issuance, to the first `Lock` transition, never the first `Close`, because `Closed` admits audit adjustments by design and a diff against pre-audit numbers would compare to figures nobody ever issued. `AS ISSUED TO <audience>` selects a specific issuance. A fifth, `AS REPORTED IN <statement_certificate>`, pins exactly the closure a published statement used, so a regulator can reproduce a filed schedule byte-for-byte years later.

### 7.5 The close as a graph

A `CloseRun` is a control object for `(book, period)` with a checklist of `CloseTask`s (vertices) and `DEPENDS_ON` edges: reconcile every bank instrument, post accruals, run depreciation, revalue monetary balances, run allocations, match intercompany, generate eliminations, roll forward, produce statements, obtain attestations, lock. Each task names the Canon rule or procedure that performs it, its evidence requirement, its approver role, and its completion attestation; the run is itself a DAG whose readiness is computed by the substrate's cycle/topological procedures. **Every close is first rehearsed on a branch** (`fa close dry-run --period 2026-03` forks a draft, executes every automated task, produces the statements, and reports the diff against the prior period and against the live book) and then executed on trunk with the same certified plan; the rehearsal's certificate is attached to the live run. Year-end `Closing`-class entries are real, immutable entries generated by the `YearEndClose` rule and they are **incremental**: each run closes the *not-yet-closed* net of every nominal account for the fiscal year (the fold of nominal legs effective in the year minus prior `Closing` legs) to the account named by the chart's `closes_to` map — retained earnings for P&L accounts, AOCI for `OCI`-class accounts, fund balance per fund dimension — so an audit adjustment, revision, or restatement posted after a close is followed by a `reclose` task that emits only the delta (`Closing` is admissible in `Closed` under `close` and in `Locked` only inside an approved case; Appendix C). FA-INV-03 clause `nominal-zero`: after the latest `Closing` entry, Σ nominal balances for the year = 0 at that known-at (`fa verify close FY2026`). **No opening-balance entries are ever posted** except `Migration` (§8.6): a real account's opening balance is the fold `EFFECTIVE < FY.start`, bounded by a `RegisterCheckpoint` at each fiscal-year boundary, so there is nothing to double count. `rollforward.retained_earnings` is a certified computation **Stock versus flow is a property of the statement line, not of the selector**: `FOR PERIOD P` restricts *flow* lines (income statement, cash flow, activity buckets) to legs effective in `P`, while *stock* lines (balance sheet, trial-balance balances, agings) fold every leg effective on or before `P.end` (`EFFECTIVE ≤ end`), which is why no opening entries exist; and income-statement definitions carry `exclude_classes: [Closing]` so the close never erases the activity it closes while the balance-sheet fold includes the closing legs so retained earnings reflects the close (§12.1). — opening retained earnings + closing entries − distributions ± `PriorPeriodAdjustment` = closing retained earnings — tied to the `EquityRollForward` statement.

### 7.6 Accruals, reversals, recurring entries, cutoff

An `Adjusting` entry may declare `auto_reverse_on: DateDay`; the kernel then posts the paired `Reversal`-class entry as a *scheduled* control effect at the later of (a) the first `ExpirySweep` tick on or after that date and (b) the `OpenPeriod` transition of the period containing `auto_reverse_on` — in case (b) the reversal is minted inside the `OpenPeriod` control batch, so a scheduled reversal never meets a `Future` period and `fa period open --dry-run` lists what it will mint — under a schedule authorization bound by the original approved plan. The schedule records the original ProposalRef, deterministic reversal rule and date, authorized effect limits and execution-authority requirement; it does not embed a future reversal envelope in the original proposal. Execution resolves the committed original, rechecks current authority, period admissibility and cancellation lineage, then creates the exact reversal under that schedule. The original approval supplies historical authorization only for its bound effects. A scheduled reversal whose target is later reversed or replaced is cancelled by lineage via a journaled `ScheduleCancelled` control effect, so an accrual can never be reversed twice. Recurring entries are a `RecurringSchedule` control object that mints proposals on schedule under a stripped `Cx` whose "today" is the tick's time evidence. Cutoff tests compare `document_date`, `effective_date`, and `recorded_at` per period boundary (Lantern §11.7).

### 7.7 Calendar arithmetic and time zones

`fa-time` implements civil dates, durations in days/months with explicit end-of-month policy (`PreserveDay`, `ClampToMonthEnd`, `LastDayStaysLast`), business-day calendars, and period generation; instants are UTC inputs admitted through TimeEvidence (§7.1a), with SubstrateMarker used only once live. The book's `time_zone_policy` governs how an instant on a document is converted to an effective civil date when a rule needs one (always explicit, always recorded on the entry as `effective_date_basis`).

### 7.8 Interim posture before valid-time contracts

Until the substrate's `ValidTimeContract` is live (Appendix H row R-VT), `effective_day` is an ordinary `Int` property; period membership, account and dimension effectivity, and relationship intervals are enforced by `fa-time`'s interval algebra in the posting kernel (same laws, same oracle). The substrate has no range indexes or range witnesses today (Appendix H R-IDX), so the posting transaction never scans — every validation is a point read on `Period`, `Account`, `DimensionValue`, a target `Entry`, or an identity-derived `vid` — and readers use the per-snapshot in-memory equality index on `effective_day`/`account_ref` where it exists; the §17 "balance read at watermark" gate assumes the tail is read through `delta_since` bounded by `max_tail_legs`, not through an index. The selectors of §7.4 are implemented by FrankenAccounting over those properties. When the contract lands, the same elements are migrated under the substrate's shadow-build/validate/activate pattern with a differential run of every §16 temporal test on both representations before the switch; the decision is a registered row, not a silent change.

---

## 8. Palimpsest: Amendments, Restatements, and Supersession (Bet A2, part 2)

A palimpsest is a manuscript scraped and written over, with the original still legible beneath. That is the exact property an accounting system needs and almost none has: the books as currently stated *and* the books as originally posted, both first-class, with the lineage between them a queryable object.

### 8.1 Entry classes

`entry_classes.toml` is the closed registry; every entry carries exactly one class, and the class determines admissibility (Appendix C), required supersession targets, required approvals, and which registers it touches.

| Class | Meaning | Supersession | Typical origin |
