|---|---|---|---|
| `Original` | An ordinary posting of a new fact | none | Intent, rule |
| `Pending` | Two-phase reservation (§5.4) | none | Intent, rule |
| `Resolution` | Posts/voids/expires a pending entry | `RESOLVES` exactly one `Pending` | Intent, sweep |
| `Reversal` | Exact negation of one target entry, same or later period | `SUPERSEDES {Reverses}` one target | Intent, auto-reverse schedule |
| `Replacement` | The corrected entry in a reversal+replacement pair | `SUPERSEDES {Replaces}` one target, must be paired with its `Reversal` in the same batch | Intent |
| `Reclassification` | Moves amounts between accounts/dimensions with no P&L effect beyond classification | `SUPERSEDES {Reclassifies}` ≥ 1 target legs | Intent, rule |
| `Adjusting` | Accruals, deferrals, estimates, period-end adjustments | optional | Rule, close |
| `Closing` | Year-end close of nominal accounts | none | `YearEndClose` rule |
| `Allocation` | Distribution under an allocation law | none; records law and basis | Rule |
| `Revaluation` | FX remeasurement/translation under an FX law | none; records law and rate documents | Rule |
| `Elimination` | Intercompany/consolidation elimination in a consolidation book | references the `INTERCOMPANY_WITH` pair | Rule |
| `Migration` | Opening balances and legacy history import | none; `migrated_from` provenance | Bulk load |
| `Revision` | Immaterial prior-period correction ("little r") | `SUPERSEDES {Revises}`; inside a `RestatementCase` of kind `Revision` | Case |
| `Restatement` | Material prior-period correction ("Big R", ASC 250 / IAS 8) | `SUPERSEDES {Restates}`; inside an approved `RestatementCase` | Case, dual control |
| `PriorPeriodAdjustment` | Adjustment to opening retained earnings for a prior-period error, posted in the current open period with `relates_to` naming the error period | inside a case | Case |
| `PeriodControl` / `SchemaControl` / `PolicyControl` | State-machine transitions, chart and registry changes | none | Controller |

`entry_classes.toml` and `admissibility.toml` jointly declare each supported `ControlPayload` discriminant's permitted entry class, arity, scope (`Book` or named `Period`), required capability and SoD row. `PeriodTransition → PeriodControl` is period-scoped; `SchemaChange → SchemaControl` and `PolicyActivation → PolicyControl` are book-scoped; `ReservationRelease → Resolution` follows the pending-release rules. Lifecycle, time, sweep-cursor, schedule-cancellation, capability, hold, erasure and migration controls each require their own explicit registered mapping and authority row. Until a row and its checker exist, that control behavior is an explicitly unsupported capability and is refused. G0 checks complete supported/unsupported classification; a gate requiring one of these behaviors cannot pass while its row/checker is absent. A generic control tag never bypasses authorization or inherits an unrelated schema-change permission.

### 8.2 Supersession lineage and netting

`SUPERSEDES` edges form a DAG rooted at original entries (FA-INV-08: acyclic; every target exists; a `Reversal` negates exactly; a `Replacement` is paired). Two derived views are maintained as registers: **as currently stated** (every entry, with superseded entries still present because their reversals net them out — the ledger never hides anything) and **as originally posted at known-at *t*** (the fold of entries recorded ≤ *t*). The **net effect** of a lineage chain is a computed object (`fa explain lineage <entry>`) listing original, reversals, replacements, and the net legs; a chain whose net is zero is a "fully reversed" fact and is reported as such. Reason codes are a registered vocabulary (`reason_codes.toml`: `DataEntryError`, `WrongAccount`, `WrongPeriod`, `WrongAmount`, `Duplicate`, `EstimateChange`, `PolicyChange`, `Fraud`, `AuditAdjustment`, `RegulatoryDirective`, `Other(text)`), so the reasons for corrections are themselves analyzable (Lantern §11.7 reports reversal-pattern anomalies by preparer and reason). `supersession_kinds.toml` fixes each kind's **netting law** (FA-INV-08 clause `net-law`): `Reverses` = exact negation of every target leg (side swap preserving native mantissas, original base valuations, law/rate/basis provenance and recorded residual assignment; never revaluation at the reversal date); `Replaces` = paired with its reversal, net = the replacement; `Reclassifies` = partial negation of the named target legs by amount plus new legs; `Restates` / `Revises` / `Corrects` = **delta** (the target stays in force; net = target + delta); `Resolves` = §5.4. An entry is the target of at most one full `Reverses` (clause `reversed-once`). Uniqueness is by identity, not by review: `vid(Reversal) = H("fa:vid:reversal:v1" ‖ target_vid)` and `vid(Replacement) = H("fa:vid:replacement:v1" ‖ target_vid)`, so two independently keyed reversals of one original collide as an `AlreadyLive` point conflict (exit `6`, citing the existing reversal) regardless of idempotency keys; reversing a reversal is explicit (`Reverses` whose target is the reversal; its own derived vid), nets to the original, and is flagged in `fa explain lineage`. Immaterial errors corrected in the current period without a case are `Adjusting` entries with `reason_code: PriorPeriodError { relates_to: PeriodRef }` so the summary of uncorrected and corrected misstatements (§11.12) can aggregate them.

`Reverse` admits only target classes with a registered exact posted-leg and application-effect negation law. A `Pending` target is refused with `VoidReserve`; an already resolved hold is handled according to its resolution outcome. Reversing `Resolution(Posts)` reverses its posted and application effects only and does not recreate the original pending reservation. `Resolution(Voids|Expires)` and zero-leg lifecycle/schema/policy/time controls are not side-swapped; the refusal names their registered corrective transition where one exists. The exhaustive target-class table must explicitly admit or refuse every class, including reversal-of-reversal; a catch-all side-swap is prohibited.

### 8.3 Restatement cases

The `Approved` case transition names the exact ordered effect manifest and the approvals validating it, but is not itself a member of the manifest it approves. Its manifest binds the existing immutable case body; the later `Posted` transition names the resulting committed EntryRefs. `IN_CASE` edges are projections of this authenticated membership, not independent assertions. A bundle asserting case-based admissibility includes the case body, approval transition, ordered manifest, member proposals and approvals needed to verify membership and the applicable SoD row. It cannot infer case approval solely from a graph edge or a case's current status.

A `RestatementCase {kind: Revision|Restatement|PriorPeriodAdjustment|PolicyChangeRetrospective, periods_affected[], disclosure: Document, authority: approvals[], status}` is the control object that makes a material correction legal in a `Locked` period. Its life cycle is `Proposed → Determined (for `Restatement`: the non-reliance determination, its date and authority — the Item 4.02 Form 8-K clock starts here, so the date is a journaled fact rather than a later reconstruction) → Rehearsed (on a branch or scratch book, with the as-reported-vs-as-restated diff certificate attached) → Approved (dual control, by principals with `restate` capability, neither of whom prepared any entry in the case) → Posted (the case's entries commit in one batch) → Disclosed (the disclosure document is attested) → Closed`. The **restatement diff certificate** is a certified computation of every affected statement line as originally reported, as restated, and the delta, with the lineage of every contributing entry — the document a company's audit committee, its auditor, and the SEC comment-letter process all need, generated rather than assembled; it also emits the opening statement of financial position at the start of the earliest comparative (IAS 1.40A's third balance sheet) and the ASC 250-10-50-7 line-item table. Retrospective application of a policy change (new revenue-recognition policy applied to comparatives) is the same machinery with `kind: PolicyChangeRetrospective` and the policy versions named.

### 8.4 Admissibility

Appendix C is the normative matrix of `Period.state × EntryClass → {Admit, AdmitWith(capability, approvals), Refuse}`. Two rules are universal: an `Original` entry is never admissible in `Closed` or `Locked`; a `Restatement` is only admissible inside an `Approved` case. The matrix is a registry (`admissibility.toml`) with a generated exhaustive test, so a new class or state cannot be added without a decision for every cell.

### 8.5 Erasure, legal holds, and retention

Amounts, accounts, dates, units, and lineage are never erased. Personal data can appear in `Party` overflow maps, `Document` bytes, and the free-text and identifier fields of entries — descriptions, leg memos, tags, external ids, `migrated_from.source_entered_by/approved_by`, `Event.attributes` — so those fields are typed **`Erasable<T>`** in Appendix A. The selected parent-digest contract covers the complete canonical encrypted field wire, including public context, nonce and ciphertext. Key destruction preserves that parent identity only while the exact immutable wire remains retained; resealing or content-key rotation produces a different representation and cannot silently replace a committed field. If a later retention or erasure policy removes ciphertext, the registered tombstone/commitment and `ReplayCompleteness` rules must state what remains verifiable: a surviving digest does not permit byte replay of missing bytes. A field that concerns no party is stored under the book key and is not erasable. Subject keys are per `Party`; erasing a party therefore requires reaching every copy of its data and every recoverable key path. A legal hold and an erasure are both control records whose plans read each other's state as a witness, so the race is a typed conflict, never a silent loss; a lawful erasure request is an `ErasureCut` control entry under the substrate's erasure-tombstone mechanism, with attributable downgrade to `Redacted` for certificates whose plaintext closure is no longer available. That mechanism is not live (Appendix H R-ERASE): until it lands, `ErasureCut` is a feature-flagged absence refused with the row cited — an append-only store cannot crypto-erase without the engine's GC cooperation, and a FrankenAccounting-level "tombstone" that left the protected plaintext reachable would be a prohibited shortcut. A `LegalHold {scope, authority, issued_by}` control entry pins every object in scope against erasure, GC, and retention expiry until released by an authorized principal; holds are visible to the auditor profile and their release is journaled. Retention floors are registered `Policy` objects subject to the applicable authority and hold; the substrate's retention tiers enforce them.

**Sealing, retry, and surviving commitments.** Idempotency is a protocol requirement, not a reason to invent a deterministic encryption mode. The initial admitted direction uses the foundation's registered randomized AEAD profile and retains one canonical prepared proposal for exact retry, with preparation durability and authorization explicitly specified. A newly supplied plaintext request may be compared with the recorded logical request only while the necessary secrets exist and the caller is authorized; after erasure, authorized outcome lookup and exact prepared-request replay remain distinct from a new semantic comparison, which must refuse if it requires destroyed secrets. A matching idempotency key never makes different content equivalent. These requirements do not admit an unfinished preparation protocol or make key rotation/erasure live before Appendix H allows them.

After authorization and identity lookup, exact equality of the validated canonical prepared request is checked before any key-requiring semantic comparison. If the requests differ, an admitted semantic retry path compares fallible, typed plaintext transcripts only within the authorized secret-handling boundary. It must propagate encoding errors and must not manufacture comparison ciphertext by resealing multiple values under a fixed nonce, emit reusable plaintext-derived fingerprints, or accept changed content solely on key identity. Equality responses can themselves test guesses; the comparison authority and observable results belong in the threat model even with randomized sealing.

Before admitting any erasable field, its versioned profile must define and authenticate the subject, type, field role, and key-epoch context on which the consumer relies, and reconcile the field commitment with the exact parent-digest transcript. A parent that authenticates ciphertext does not by itself supply every standalone-field context guarantee. The threat model inventories all surviving public values derived from private input, including intent identifiers and provenance digests: an unkeyed hash of a low-entropy private request can preserve a guessing oracle after encryption keys are destroyed. Deterministic tags additionally expose equality and permit candidate matching when the observer has a sealing oracle under the same key; secret salt alone does not remove that condition. No custom KDF-plus-AEAD composition inherits a SIV security claim by its name, and finite-width nonce derivation is not a guarantee of impossible collisions. Admission requires explicit threat boundaries and replay, changed-content, context-substitution, rotation, and post-erasure vectors; those checks do not substitute for review of a new cryptographic construction. Until that contract and its checker exist, the corresponding privacy/retry capability is refused rather than advertised as enforced.

### 8.6 Migration from legacy systems

A migration is a `Migration`-class batch with: the legacy trial balance as a `Document`, opening balances posted into the `Opening` period, optional legacy journal detail imported as `Migration` entries whose `effective_date` is the legacy effective date and whose `migrated_from` carries the legacy journal id, entered-by, entered-at, and approved-by fields (the AICPA ADS columns map one-to-one), and a `MigrationAttestation` by the principal responsible that the imported detail folds to the legacy trial balance (the kernel computes and the attestation signs the digest). Legacy "as known at" is lost by definition and the book says so: `AS KNOWN AT` any instant before the migration returns `BeforeBookGenesis`, never a reconstruction. **No double counting** (FA-INV-07 clause `migration-disjoint`): the opening-balance entry is effective on `opening_date`, the first day of a fiscal year, in that year's `Opening` period (`[FY start, FY start + 1)`); imported detail is admitted only into `Historical` periods inside an open `MigrationRun` and only in one of three declared modes — `OpeningOnly`, `PeriodBalances(periods[])` (one entry per elapsed period tied to that period's legacy trial balance; the mid-year go-live case), or `FullDetail` (legacy journal entries as detail, each in a `Historical` period at its legacy date; the run declares a contiguous run of `Historical` periods ending at the book's opening date with each period's trial balance as of its end, plus `detail_base`, the trial balance at the start of the first declared period, empty for an import from legacy inception). Fold inclusion is decided once by the migration predicate that every fold applies. A finalized `PeriodBalances` run's entries, opening and per-period alike, count in trunk folds, and its `Finalize` ties out cumulatively: the opening entries to the opening trial balance, and the opening plus every elapsed period through `p` to `p`'s trial balance. A `FullDetail` run's `Finalize` ties one cumulative chain, `detail_base` plus the detail through `p` to each `p`'s trial balance. The chain walks the fiscal calendar's period order (each year's `Opening`, `P01`…`P12`, adjustment periods, then `Closing`), so detail in the special periods between declared periods, such as a legacy year-end close, is folded without those periods being declared. A declared `Normal` period's trial balance is pre-closing. Unless the run declares its detail the sole source of the opening balances, its opening entries tie to the opening trial balance, which must equal the chain at the run's opening date (the fiscal-year start). Its opening entries count, and a detail entry counts iff it is dated on or after the run's opening date: earlier detail is summarized by the opening entry and stays journaled for drill-down and lineage, so it is never counted twice. With `sole_opening_source`, the run holds no opening-balance entry, no opening trial balance and no `detail_base`, and every detail entry counts. The book's opening date only bounds the declared periods. The `MigrationAttestation` names the mode and the per-period tie-out digests. **Staging is in the same engine and invisible until activation**: a `MigrationRun` in state `Open` makes its `Historical` periods — and every entry committed into them — invisible to ordinary coordinates and excluded from registers through the same visibility conjunct expiry uses (visible iff the run is `Finalized`); chunks commit durably as they arrive, a failed or invalid final chunk moves the run to `Abandoned` (its entries stay journaled and permanently invisible; nothing is deleted), a retry is a new run whose entry vids include the run id, and `Finalize` is one control record that atomically activates the whole run after the tie-out attestation verifies. **Open items** (ruling 3733): a migrated leg is an open item exactly when the migration predicate selects its entry (its run `Finalized`, the entry counting), so a settlement against a staged or abandoned run's leg, or against legacy detail the opening entry summarizes, is refused `SETTLE_TARGET` with the run's state read as a point witness; an opening receivable meant to be cleared invoice by invoice is imported as one opening leg per open invoice, which is the importer's shape and needs nothing more of the kernel. **Interim posture (Appendix H R-BR):** a `Migration` chunk carries no `settles`; legacy applications inside a run are refused `SETTLE_SHAPE`, and each legacy open item is imported net of its legacy applications until a staged branch (or fa-omi's staged applications) can carry them with open amounts proven equal to the fold.

---

## 9. Docket: Evidence, Provenance, and Custody (Bet A3, part 1)

### 9.1 Documents

A `Document` is a content-addressed object: `{oid (BLAKE3 of canonical bytes through the substrate's keyed ObjectId), kind: {Invoice, Bill, Receipt, Contract, BankStatement, Check(image), WireConfirmation, Remittance, PurchaseOrder, ReceivingReport, Rate, Confirmation, Representation, BoardMinutes, CourtOrder, Memo, Email, Spreadsheet, Image, Disclosure, Workpaper, Other}, mime, size, storage: Inline{object} | External{locator, fetch_proof}, parsed: Option<ParsedProjection>, redaction: Option<RedactionContractRef>, retrieved_by, retrieved_at (recorded), custody: ImportReceiptRef}`. Inline storage puts the bytes in the book's commit stream (erasure-coded, encrypted, deduplicated by digest); every document also carries a **public commitment** `doc_commit = BLAKE3("fa:doc:v1" ‖ kind ‖ length ‖ chunk_root)` where `chunk_root` is the root of a Merkle tree over fixed 4 KiB chunks of the canonical bytes — the substrate's keyed `ObjectId` needs `K_oid` and is only a locator, so every evidence list, bundle, receipt (`ImportReceipt.digest_at_receipt = doc_commit`), and certificate references `doc_commit`, which a verifier recomputes from the bytes alone, and a redacted production discloses a subset of chunks with their proofs (sub-chunk redaction is labeled `disclosure_only`, never `proven`); external storage records a locator and a `fetch_proof` (the digest observed at import plus the import receipt), and a later fetch that does not match the digest is a finding, not a silent substitution. Parsing (a `camt.053` statement into `BankLine`s; an invoice PDF's extracted fields) is a derived projection with its parser version recorded; the bytes are the evidence, the projection is a convenience. Bulk evidence (a year of statements) is bounded per document and per book by registry rows and reported in the byte-economy metrics.

### 9.2 The provenance closure of an entry

The closure is the set an outsider needs to verify "why": the entry's evidence documents (`EVIDENCED_BY`), its REA event and the rule version that derived it (`DERIVED_FROM`, `GENERATED_BY`, with the rule's inputs digest), its principal and session, its approvals, every rate or basis document its arithmetic consumed, the allocation driver tables, and — for a resolution, reversal, or restatement — the target entries. FA-INV-05: every committed entry's closure is strongly retained for the retention floor and is enumerable by `fa explain evidence <entry>`; an entry whose `origin` names an unregistered source is unencodable. **Evidence minimums are a registry, not a vacuous check** (`evidence_policy.toml`; FA-INV-05 clause `evidence-minimum`): an `Intent`-origin `Original`, `Adjusting`, or `Reclassification` entry requires at least one document of an admissible kind for its rule family *or* an explicit `ManualAssertion` attestation by the preparer carrying a registered reason code ("no document exists because …"); a `Rule`-origin entry inherits its event's evidence, and an event with no document is refused; control classes require their control record and nothing else; a draft may hold intents with missing evidence in state `NeedsEvidence`, but promotion refuses them. **External documents retain bytes by default**: `External` storage keeps a canonical inline copy at the retention floor and the locator is a convenience; a book may declare `external_evidence_policy = ByReferenceOnly(accepted_by: principal)` as an explicit, attested weakening that downgrades every dependent certificate's completeness grade to `VerifiableIfArtifactsSupplied`, and a fetch whose bytes no longer match `doc_commit` is a finding. **Evidence attached after commit is a new object**: `AttachEvidence` creates an immutable `SupplementalEvidence { entry_ref, documents, role, attached_by, recorded_seq }`; the entry's original list is unchanged, `fa explain evidence` shows both with their sequences, and a certificate names which closure it used (original-only or original-plus-supplements as of its known-at). The closure's digest is part of the entry's certificate, so an agent can hand a reviewer one hash that covers the entry and everything it rests on.

### 9.3 Chain of custody

An `ImportReceipt {source: {Upload(principal), Connector(name, version), Email(message_id), Scan(device)}, received_at (recorded), digest_at_receipt, transformations[]}` is created for every document the book ingests, before any parsing; the chain from receipt through every derived projection is edges with versions. Every document carries a **provenance grade** derived from its receipt chain — `ThirdPartySigned`, `Connector(transcript)`, `HumanUpload`, `AgentUpload`, `Generated` — shown by `fa explain evidence`, in bundles, and to Lantern. An entry whose only evidence is `AgentUpload` or `Generated` is **self-sourced**: the Agent profile may commit it only on a draft, promotion requires an approver's `Reviewed { against }` attestation on each such document, and `jet.self_sourced_evidence` runs on every draft before promotion. Parsed text projections served to agents are marked `untrusted_content` so an instruction embedded in an invoice cannot pass as a command. Productions to third parties (a litigation production, a regulator's request) are `Bundle`s (§13.2) whose manifest records what was produced, under which capability, with which redactions, and the recipient's acknowledgment attestation if returned.

### 9.4 Attestations

An `Attestation {claim, subject_refs[], principal_ref, signature: {scheme, key_id, bytes}, signed_digest, evidence_refs[], recorded_at}` is a signed statement about objects: a preparer's "I prepared this", a reviewer's "I reviewed this against the invoice", a controller's "this reconciliation is complete", an external auditor's "we have examined these bundles", a bank's confirmation. The **balance assertion** (from plain-text accounting, upgraded) is an attestation whose claim is `RegisterEquals { cell_key, amount, as_of_effective, evaluated_known_at, fold_digest }` with the evidence (a bank statement) attached: it binds the cell's coordinate and the fold digest at the sequence where it was evaluated, so the attestation itself is immutable and can never become false. Drift is a separate, head-following **reconciliation monitor**: the kernel re-evaluates every live assertion's cell at head after each commit touching it and emits a `BalanceAssertionDrift` finding citing the attestation and the entries recorded since — a later entry into an attested period is admissible (the books are the books) but never silent. `Instrument.reconciled_through` is additionally a **soft lock**: a leg on the mirrored account with `effective_date ≤ reconciled_through` requires the `reconcile` capability and emits a finding citing the attestation, so an agent cannot wander into a reconciled month by accident. Signature schemes are the substrate's authenticated-type profiles; external signatures (an auditor's PKI certificate) are stored as documents with the verification record.

### 9.5 Bank statements and external records

`fa-formats` parses ISO 20022 `camt.053`/`camt.052`, BAI2, OFX/QFX, MT940, and declared CSV layouts into `BankLine {instrument_ref, booking_date, value_date, amount, counterparty, reference, statement_oid, line_ordinal, line_digest}` vertices under `PARSED_FROM` edges to the statement document. Statement sequence continuity (opening balance equals prior closing; statement numbers contiguous) is checked at import and gaps are findings. Lines match legs (§5.7); unmatched lines are the raw material of the bank-reconciliation procedure and of the kiting and lapping detectors.

### 9.6 Redaction contracts, retention, holds

A `RedactionContract` on a document class declares which fields or byte ranges are privileged and under which capability they are visible; the substrate's redaction (§12.6 there) hides the bytes while the document's digest, size, kind, and its evidentiary edges remain visible, and the proof that a redacted document is the one an entry cites still verifies (the digest is over the unredacted canonical bytes; the redacted projection carries a redaction proof). Until that lands (Appendix H R-RED), privileged document *bytes* are stored in a separate `PrivilegedDocument` vertex class whose reads require the `privileged` caveat, evaluated by Steward before query construction — never as a result filter. Amounts, dates, and accounts are never redactable. Retention and legal holds are §8.5.

### 9.7 Who read what

Every read under an `Auditor`, `Regulator`, `Investigator`, or `Production` capability (§14.4) records an `AccessEvent {capability_ref, principal_ref, snapshot_seq, query_digest, objects_touched_digest, recorded_at}` in the system graph — durably, as one maintenance-class publication committed *before* the first protected byte leaves the daemon (a crash before that commit means nothing escaped; a crash after it over-records, which is labeled and acceptable; a cancelled stream is recorded as `partial`), priced as its own Appendix G row rather than hidden inside a "zero-write" read class, with reads of the access log itself recorded once per query rather than per row — complete for the external profiles, for every read of a `Document` under a redaction contract or of a `Finding`, and for every read by a principal who writes in the same session; sampled only for register and statement reads by internal profiles, because "who looked at this before it changed" must always be answerable. The access log is itself an append-only, queryable graph (`MATCH (a:AccessEvent)-[:READ]->(d:Document {kind: 'Contract'}) WHERE a.recorded_at > $t RETURN a.principal`), which is what a privilege-log or a regulator's own audit of the examination needs.

---

## 10. Canon: Posting Rules and Typed Intents (Bet A6, part 1)

### 10.1 Why a rules language

Every serious ledger has one: SAP's account determination, Oracle's subledger accounting rules, Formance's Numscript, Beancount's plugins, the thousand-line stored procedures that generate entries in a bank's core. They are where the accounting policy actually lives, and they are uniformly unversioned, side-effecting, untestable in isolation, and invisible to the auditor. Canon makes the rule a first-class, content-addressed, deterministic, *total* object: a rule version is a `RuleVersion` vertex; an entry records which version derived it from which event; re-evaluating the version over the event reproduces the entry byte-for-byte; and a rule that could not be evaluated (a missing rate, an unknown account) fails closed with a typed error instead of posting a guess.

### 10.2 Typed intents

An **intent** is the agent-facing unit of work: a typed, validated request whose dry-run plan is deterministic and whose commit is idempotent. `intents.toml` registers each intent's schema, required capability, admissible entry classes, and which Canon rule family it invokes. The 1.0 catalog (normative fields in Appendix B):

| Intent | What it does | Rule family |
|---|---|---|
| `PostEntry` | Post explicit legs (the escape hatch; everything below could be expressed as it) | none |
| `PostBatch` | Atomic list of intents | none |
| `RecordEvent` | Record an REA event and let the book's rules derive entries | event → entries |
| `RecordInvoice` / `RecordBill` | AR/AP originals with tax and dimension handling | sales/purchase |
| `ApplyPayment` / `ApplyReceipt` | Settlement against open items (`settles`) | application |
| `Reserve` / `PostReserve` / `VoidReserve` | Two-phase (§5.4) | two-phase |
| `Accrue` / `Defer` (with `auto_reverse_on`) | Adjusting entries | adjusting |
| `Reclass` | Move amounts between accounts/dimensions | reclassification |
| `Reverse` / `Replace` | Reversal; reversal+replacement pair | supersession |
| `Allocate` | Distribute under an allocation law with a driver table | allocation |
| `Revalue` / `Translate` | FX period-end (§12.4) | revaluation |
| `Depreciate` / `Amortize` | Fixed-asset and intangible schedules | schedules |
| `MatchBank` / `ConfirmMatch` / `AssertBalance` | Reconciliation (§5.7, §9.4) | reconciliation |
| `OpenAccount` / `CloseAccount` / `PromoteToSummary` / `ImportChart` | Chart control | schema control |
| `OpenPeriod` / `SoftClose` / `ClosePeriod` / `LockPeriod` / `ReopenPeriod` | Period control (§7.3) | period control |
| `RunClose` (dry-run / execute) | Close orchestration (§7.5) | close |
| `OpenRestatementCase` / `RehearseCase` / `ApproveCase` / `PostCase` / `DiscloseCase` | §8.3 | case |
| `AttachEvidence` / `Attest` / `ImportDocument` | Docket | evidence |
| `Migrate` | §8.6 | migration |
| `IssueCapability` / `Revoke` / `LegalHold` / `ReleaseHold` / `ErasureCut` | Steward (§14) | governance |
| `ForkDraft` / `PromoteDraft` / `DiscardDraft` / `ForkScenario` / `DiffBranches` | A6 (§15.2) | branches |

Every intent returns the same envelope: `{outcome, entry_refs[], plan_certificate, register_deltas[], warnings[], next_actions[]}`. Every intent accepts `dry_run: true` and returns the plan without committing; intents whose class is irreversible-in-practice (`LockPeriod`, `PostCase`, `ErasureCut`, `Migrate`) *require* a prior dry-run certificate to be presented (`confirm_plan: <certificate_id>`) so an agent cannot lock a period with one call it never previewed.

### 10.3 The rule language

Canon is a small, statically typed, total, pure language whose programs map a typed event (plus explicitly declared inputs: rates, tables, the effective date, the chart at that date) to a list of candidate legs. Its design rules: no loops except folds over declared inputs whose bound is the input's length (so lot relief and allocation over *n* targets are total without a static constant); no recursion; no I/O; no clock; no entropy; no string-to-number parsing at runtime (parsing is a declared input transformation with a parser version); every arithmetic operation names its rounding law or inherits the unit's default; every account reference is by code resolved at the entry's effective date; pattern-matching over event kinds is exhaustive. A sketch:

```text
rule sales_invoice v7 for event Sale {
  inputs { tax_table: TaxTable@oid, revenue_map: AccountMap@oid }
  let gross = event.lines.sum(l => l.quantity * l.unit_price round HalfEven)
  let tax   = tax_table.apply(event.jurisdiction, event.lines) round TaxAuthority(US-CA)
  debit  "1200-AR"                      gross + tax   dims { customer = event.party }
  credit revenue_map[event.product_class] gross        dims { product = event.product }
  credit "2300-SalesTaxPayable"          tax           dims { jurisdiction = event.jurisdiction }
  effective event.invoice_date
  evidence event.documents
}
```

The compiler produces a content-addressed typed IR (`RuleVersion.ir_oid`), type-checks units (a `USD` amount cannot be added to a `JPY` amount; a quantity times a price yields the price's unit), proves totality by construction, and records the rule's static dependency set (which accounts, tables, laws, and units it may reference) so a chart change that removes an account a live rule references is refused with the rule's name. The IR's compile target is the substrate's deterministic UDF VM (Appendix H row R-VM); the interim evaluator is a tree-walking interpreter over the same IR under a stripped `Cx`, with the IR frozen so compiled and interpreted execution are differentially tested on every rule's golden corpus. Rules are versioned by content and *activated* by a `PolicyControl` entry naming the version and its effective date range; the same event evaluated under two active books (US GAAP and tax) may bind different rule versions (§12.2).

### 10.4 Dry-run semantics

A dry run executes steps 1–6 of §5.1 on a pinned snapshot and returns the plan: candidate legs with resolved accounts and dimensions, per-unit and base totals, the period decision with the matrix cell cited, the SoD decision, escrow rights required and available, the evidence closure heads, register deltas at head, warnings (a round amount, an unusual account for this rule, an effective date in a soft-closed period, `PossibleDuplicate` when an entry with equal legs, amount, party, and date within *k* days exists — cited, and a refusal under the Agent profile unless `--allow-duplicate <reason>` — and `CutoffDrift` when `effective_date` and `document_date` straddle a period boundary or differ by more than `cutoff_tolerance_days`), and a plan certificate pinning the snapshot, rule versions, laws, and registry epochs. Committing with `confirm_plan` re-validates the witnesses; if anything the plan depended on changed, the commit is refused with `PlanInvalidated {changed: [...]}` and a fresh plan, never silently re-planned.

### 10.5 Rule testing

Every rule version ships a golden corpus (`rules/<name>/golden/*.toml`: event → expected legs) and property tests the compiler generates for free: every output of every rule satisfies the balance law (it must, by construction — the compiler refuses a rule whose debit and credit expressions cannot be proven equal per unit, and emits a native residual leg only where the rule names its account and law and proves the exact pre-rounding balance premise and the bound derived from the law's relevant rounding operations. Half-family operations use the sum of half-ulp bounds; directed and coarser-quantum operations use their registered bounds (§6.2). Base-valuation residuals use §5.2's recorded absorption. No residual may absorb a difference in exact pre-rounding values), every output is in the rule's declared account set, and evaluation is byte-deterministic across platforms. Rule changes are reviewed as diffs of IR and of golden outputs.

### 10.6 Agents authoring rules

Rules are data an agent may propose (`fa rule propose --file`), test on a draft against historical events (`fa rule backtest --rule x@v8 --events 2025-Q1 --compare x@v7`, which reports every entry that would differ), and submit for activation under the `rule_change` capability with dual control. An activated rule's provenance names the proposing agent's program, model, and session, so "which model wrote the revenue rule that caused the restatement" is a query the system answers.

---

## 11. Lantern: Forensic and Audit Analytics (Bet A4)

### 11.1 Procedures are registered, deterministic, certified computations

`procedures.toml` registers every analytic: its id, inputs (coordinate, scope predicate, parameters with defaults), the claim class of its output (`statistical` for every score and threshold test; `invariant`-derived only for exact reconciliations), its assumptions, its CGSE tie-break policy for rankings, its complexity bound (witness-locked in CI), and its evidence row (`FA-EVID-*`). A procedure runs under a stripped `Cx` over a pinned snapshot and emits `Finding`s (§11.8) plus a certificate; the same inputs produce the same findings bit-for-bit, so an opposing expert can rerun the exact procedure. Thresholds are decision cards with pinned deterministic defaults (Nigrini's conformity ranges, the AS 2401 heuristics' parameters) and may be tuned per engagement with the tuning recorded.

### 11.2 Journal-entry testing (AS 2401 / ISA 240 mechanized)

`jet.unusual_accounts` (entries to seldom-used accounts, measured by the account's posting frequency distribution over the lookback), `jet.unusual_preparers` (principals who rarely post, or post outside their role's account set), `jet.period_end_timing` (entries recorded in the last *k* days of a period or after `SoftClose`), `jet.post_closing` (entries recorded after `SoftClose` with effective dates in the period, plus `Intent`-origin `Original`/`Adjusting` entries in a consolidation book with no `DERIVED_FROM` event — the top-side population), `jet.thin_descriptions` (empty, short, or templated descriptions; near-duplicate descriptions by edit distance), `jet.round_amounts` (round-number and consistent-ending-digit entries), `jet.manual_vs_rule` (`origin: Intent` entries in account families normally fed by rules), `jet.weekend_holiday` (recorded on non-business days per the book's calendar; parameterized by `Principal.kind`, human by default — agents post around the clock), `jet.self_approved` (preparer = approver; impossible for the classes `sod_policy.toml` covers, where a hit proves a capability misconfiguration, and a control finding for uncovered classes), `jet.large_round_reversals`, `jet.suspense_aging` (balances in clearing/suspense accounts by age), `jet.intercompany_unmatched`. Each emits findings with the exact entries, the statistic, and the population baseline.

### 11.3 Digit and number analytics

`benford.first_digit|second_digit|first_two_digits` (chi-square, MAD against the conformity ranges, Z per digit; population and scope stated), `numbers.round_amount_density`, `numbers.threshold_splitting` (clusters of amounts just below an approval or reporting threshold, grouped by party and window — the structuring detector; thresholds come from the book's approval policy and from a registered table including the CTR rule as applied — cash transactions aggregated per conductor per business day across instruments exceeding $10,000 (31 CFR 1010.311/.313) — and the SAR thresholds; `BankLine`/`Event` therefore carry `cash: bool` and a `CONDUCTED_BY` party edge), `numbers.relative_size_factor` (largest-to-second-largest ratio per party), `duplicates.same_same_same|same_same_different` (amount/party/date and amount/party/different-date), `duplicates.near` (amount within a tolerance and description similarity), `numbers.sequence_gaps` (gaps in external invoice/check sequences — the completeness assertion's mechanical half).

### 11.4 Graph procedures

Over the ledger graph and the `PAID` money-flow projection: `graph.related_party_exposure` (temporal closure of `OWNS`/`CONTROLS`/`RELATED_TO` as of each entry's effective date, intersected with counterparties; total exposure per related party), `graph.circular_flows` (simple cycles in the money-flow graph within a window, weighted by amount — round-tripping), `graph.layering` (paths of length ≥ 3 through intermediaries with ≥ 90% pass-through within *d* days), `graph.concentration` (betweenness and flow centrality of parties and instruments), `graph.communities` (Louvain/Leiden over flows to surface clusters of apparently unrelated parties that transact only with each other), `graph.kiting` (temporal cycles across bank instruments where a deposit's funding check clears after the deposit is drawn — modeled on `BankLine` value dates and the float), `graph.ponzi_motif` (inflows from new investors in window *w* funding outflows to earlier investors, with the new-money-to-redemptions ratio by period; investor cohorts by first-inflow date), `graph.shell_signature` (parties with no documents, no employees, address or bank shared with another party, and pass-through flows), `graph.ultimate_beneficiary` (max-flow decomposition of where money finally rests). Every graph procedure names the `fnx` algorithm and its CGSE policy in its certificate.

### 11.5 Tracing laws

Constructive-trust tracing through commingled accounts is a registered algorithm family, not an analyst's spreadsheet: `trace.libr` (lowest intermediate balance rule: the claimant's traceable amount in an account never exceeds the lowest balance between deposit and claim), `trace.fifo`, `trace.lifo`, `trace.pro_rata` (the *Cunningham v. Brown* pro-rata sharing used in Ponzi distributions), `trace.restitution_order` (a parameterized law set by the court's order). `trace.fifo` is Clayton's rule (*Devaynes v Noble, Clayton's Case* (1816)); `trace.libr` names the two parameters experts actually dispute — `intraday_order ∈ {StatementOrdinal, ValueDate, WithdrawalsFirst, DepositsFirst}` and `replenishment ∈ {Never, OnEvidence(docs)}` (Restatement (Third) of Restitution §59). Distribution methods are registered beside tracing, because the receiver's work product is the investor-level schedule: `ponzi.net_investment` (per investor, cash in − cash out; net winners and net losers; fictitious profits — the net-equity method of *In re Bernard L. Madoff Investment Securities LLC*, 654 F.3d 229 (2d Cir. 2011)), `distribution.rising_tide` (*SEC v. Huber*, 702 F.3d 903 (7th Cir. 2012)), and `distribution.pro_rata` (*Cunningham v. Brown*), each with investor cohorts by first-inflow date and the resulting clawback schedule. Each takes a source set (tainted inflows), a window, and an instrument set, and emits a traced-amount schedule per instrument and per period with the full path evidence — every `BankLine` and leg along the way — and a certificate. Two experts applying `trace.libr` to the same book with the same parameters get the same schedule; a dispute is then about the parameters, which the certificate names.

### 11.6 Solvency and lookback analysis

`solvency.balance_sheet_test` (assets vs liabilities at fair-value adjustments supplied as documents, at a point in time, `AS KNOWN AT` optionally pinned to what management knew), `solvency.cash_flow_test` (ability to pay debts as they come due over a horizon, from the aging and commitments), `lookback.preferences` (11 U.S.C. §547(b): transfers within 90 days, or 1 year for insiders under the §101(31) closure via `RELATED_TO`; insolvency presumed in the 90 days under §547(f) and testable with `solvency.*`; **defenses computed, not assumed** — subsequent-new-value netting (§547(c)(4)), ordinary-course statistics (§547(c)(2): days-to-pay and payment-method distributions in the preference window versus a parameterized baseline window), contemporaneous exchange (§547(c)(1)); output is net exposure per creditor), `lookback.fraudulent_transfer` (§548: two-year window; UVTA §9: four years, or one year from discovery for actual fraud; reasonably-equivalent-value documents; the badges of fraud as findings), `damages.but_for` (a `Scenario` branch with the counterfactual intents applied; the delta of statements between the branch and trunk is the damages schedule, certified).

### 11.7 Reconciliation, cutoff, aging, matching, sampling

`recon.bank` (unmatched lines and legs, timing differences, outstanding items by age), `recon.subledger_control` (FA-INV-12 as a report, with the detail that differs when the invariant is violated by an external feed), `recon.intercompany` (unmatched pairs and FX differences), `cutoff.period_boundary` (document date vs effective date vs recorded time around each boundary), `aging.ar|ap|inventory` (buckets by effective date and open amount), `match.three_way` (PO, receipt, invoice documents and legs), `reversal.patterns` (reversal rates by preparer, reason, account, and month), `sample.mus` (monetary-unit sampling with a declared seed, interval, and expected misstatement; the sample is reproducible from the certificate), `sample.random|stratified` (seeded), `confirm.coverage` (which balances have external confirmation attestations). Further registered procedures: `recon.lapping` (receipts whose remitter ≠ the settled invoice's party; application-lag distributions by preparer), `completeness.unrecorded_liabilities` (disbursements recorded after period end with `document_date` ≤ period end and no accrual effective in the period), `events.subsequent` (`FOR PERIOD P AS KNOWN AT period_end` versus `AS KNOWN AT report_date`, by class and reason — the bitemporal model's most direct audit use), `party.master_changes` (bank-identifier or address changes on a `Party` followed by payments within *d* days — the business-email-compromise vector), `payroll.ghost` (employees sharing a bank instrument or address, lacking HR documents), `expense.capitalization` (capital-asset legs whose evidence and vendor class are operating in nature; round top-side capitalizations), and `graph.identity_resolution` (shared tax ids, bank accounts, addresses, and phones across parties, emitted as `SAME_AS` *candidate* findings, never auto-merged).

### 11.8 Findings

A `Finding {procedure_id, version, parameters_digest, coordinate, severity: {Info, Low, Medium, High, Critical}, statistic, population, cites[], narrative_template, status: {Open, Reviewed, Dismissed(reason, principal), Escalated(case_ref)}, certificate_ref}` is a journaled vertex with `CITES` edges to every entry, leg, party, document, register, or attestation it rests on. Findings are immutable; review and dismissal are new control effects with lineage, so "which findings did the prior auditor dismiss and why" survives. Workpaper export (`fa workpaper export --finding F --format md|json|csv`) produces a stable-numbered document (`FA-<book>-WP-<seq>`) whose every reference is a verifiable handle.

### 11.9 Claim typing and calibration

A detector's output is `statistical` evidence. Its `FA-EVID-*` row names the population, the selection rule, the assumptions (Benford applies to naturally occurring, multi-order-of-magnitude, non-assigned numbers — invoice numbers are excluded by rule), the false-positive expectation, and the deterministic fallback. Learned scores (an anomaly model) are admissible only as advisory features on a decision card with the analytic procedure as the pinned fallback, and never as a finding's sole basis (A5; the substrate's §1 constraint 10 inherited). A finding is never an accusation; it is a reproducible observation with evidence.

### 11.10 Synthetic corpora and ground truth

`fa-gen` generates synthetic books with labeled scenarios — a clean retailer; a retailer with channel stuffing and bill-and-hold; a bank with kiting across three instruments; an investment vehicle running a Ponzi scheme with cohorts; a shell network with layering and structuring; a company with a WorldCom-style capitalization of expenses and top-side entries; a payroll with ghost employees — each with the planted facts recorded, so every Lantern procedure has precision/recall measured on known truth, reported as `FA-EVID` rows beside the published thresholds. The generator is deterministic by seed and is also the benchmark fixture generator (§17).

### 11.11 Audit assertions → mechanisms

| Assertion | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| Existence / occurrence | Every leg's entry carries evidence (FA-INV-05); confirmations as attestations; `sample.mus` with evidence pull |
| Completeness | Sequence-gap procedures; statement continuity at import; the ADS export's inclusion proofs for the full range; cutoff tests |
| Accuracy / valuation | Exact arithmetic under named laws (A5); rate documents; allocation driver tables; FX laws; certified recomputation |
| Cutoff | `cutoff.period_boundary`; effective vs document vs recorded time |
| Classification / presentation | Strict chart types; statement definitions as data; reclassification lineage |
| Rights and obligations | Instruments `HELD_AT`; contract documents; related-party closure |
| Authorization (controls) | SoD at commit; approvals bound to digests; capability log; `jet.self_approved` must be empty |

### 11.12 Engagement objects (mechanical, in-core; judgments outside)

The §0.2 boundary excludes *materiality judgment*; it does not exclude the mechanics an auditor needs, which are absent from every GL and therefore end in a spreadsheet. An `Engagement { firm_principal, kind: {Audit, Review, Examination, Investigation, Litigation}, scope_periods, materiality: {overall, performance, clearly_trivial} (parameters, never judgments), report_release_date?, documentation_completion_date?, capability_ref, workpaper_index }` lives in the system graph. A `ProposedAdjustment` is a draft entry authored under the Auditor capability with `status: Proposed | Booked(entry_ref) | Passed(reason, principal)`; `audit.sud` aggregates `Passed` items and reason-coded out-of-period corrections (`PriorPeriodError`, §8.2) per statement line under both SAB 108 methods (rollover and iron curtain) against the engagement's materiality parameters, with prior-year carry-forward — the summary of uncorrected misstatements, generated. Engagement workpapers and findings are append-only after `documentation_completion_date` (AS 1215.15: within 45 days of the report release date); later additions carry `added_after_completion`. `AttestationClaim` gains `Represented { claims[], as_of }` for management representations; `Confirmed` is audit-grade only when the confirmation document's `ImportReceipt.source` principal holds the engagement's capability (AS 2310: the auditor controls the confirmation). `sample.mus` names its inputs (population book value, tolerable misstatement, expected misstatement, risk of incorrect acceptance) and gains `evaluate` (projected misstatement and upper misstatement limit under the AICPA sampling-guide factors). The `Engagement` is the unit a firm's independence, scope, and documentation rules attach to; none of its objects alters a committed amount.

---

## 12. Statements and Concordat: Reports, Multi-Book, FX, and Consolidation

### 12.1 Statement definitions as data; reports as certified computations

A `StatementDefinition {kind: {TrialBalance, BalanceSheet, IncomeStatement, CashFlowIndirect, CashFlowDirect, EquityRollForward, Aging, GLDetail, Custom}, lines[{code, label, mapping: AccountSetExpr | Formula(lines), sign, subtotal_of}], comparatives, presentation_unit, rounding_law, version}` is a journaled, content-addressed object; a company's balance-sheet layout under US GAAP, the same under IFRS, and a bank's call-report schedule are three definitions. A report run is `(definition@oid, coordinate, parameters) → rows + certificate`; rows are computed from registers (verified against the fold at the register's watermark, with the tail folded exactly) and the certificate pins definition, coordinate, registry epochs, laws, rate documents, and the register checkpoints used. Reports are pure: the same certificate replays to the same bytes (FA-INV-11). The indirect cash-flow statement is a definition over account *movements* with declared non-cash adjustments; the direct method uses the money-flow projection; both are certified. Presentation rounding (thousands, millions) is a law and a declared residual line, so a presented statement foots exactly. **Mapping partition (FA-INV-04 clause `mapping-partition`).** A `BalanceSheet`, `IncomeStatement`, or trial-balance-derived definition is refused at definition time unless its line mappings *partition* the leaf accounts of the types it covers — no unmapped leaf, none mapped twice — at every effective date in its comparatives; `fa statement lint` lists offenders, because a leaf missing from a mapping is the most common reporting error in practice. Two further kinds: `RollForward { opening = fold EFFECTIVE < start, activity buckets by EntryClass × reason_code × rule family, closing }` — the lead schedule every auditor requests first (fixed assets, debt, allowances, equity) — and `CashFlowClassification`: every cash leg is classed Operating/Investing/Financing from its counter-legs' accounts under the definition's map, mixed entries split under a registered allocation law, so the direct and indirect statements reconcile to the cash register by construction. `tieout.filing(<xbrl instance | document>)` compares every reported fact to a certified row and emits exceptions.

### 12.2 Multi-book

A `Book` per basis (US GAAP, IFRS, tax, statutory, management) shares entities, parties, instruments, units, and calendars (statutory may differ) and has its own chart or a mapped chart, its own rule activations, its own periods, and its own registers. An `Event` posts to every book whose rules bind it; the entries carry the book id and are independent facts with shared provenance. Cross-book reconciliation (`recon.book_to_book`) explains every difference by rule version (depreciation method, lease treatment) as a certified bridge — the GAAP-to-tax reconciliation an auditor asks for, generated. A `Book` is `mode: Full` or `mode: AdjustmentLayer(of: BookRef)`: a layer book's registers are the parent's ⊕ the layer's own entries, so a tax or statutory basis is kept as book-to-basis *differences* only (the pattern of SAP extension ledgers and Oracle adjustment-only secondary ledgers), each reason-coded (Schedule M-1/M-3 category; temporary versus permanent), and `recon.book_to_book` is the layer's fold by reason — the basis-difference schedule ASC 740 starts from. A layer's periods and closes are its own; a parent restatement propagates to the layer's `AS CURRENTLY STATED` view automatically and appears on the layer's close checklist.

### 12.3 Intercompany and consolidation

Intercompany entries declare `pair_key`; the kernel matches pairs across entities' books (`INTERCOMPANY_WITH`) at commit when both sides exist and reports unmatched pairs continuously (FA-INV-13 is a synchronous constraint on *declared* pairs: amounts match under the FX law or the difference is posted to the registered intercompany-difference account by the matching rule). Consolidation is a `ConsolidationRun` on a branch of the consolidation book: aggregate the entities' statements under the ownership graph as of the period end (valid-time `OWNS {pct}`), generate `Elimination`-class entries by rule (intercompany balances and transactions, investment-in-subsidiary vs equity, unrealized profit in inventory), compute non-controlling interest and the cumulative translation adjustment, produce the consolidated statements with a certificate naming every entity's statement certificate, and promote to trunk under the controller's approval. Every consolidation is reproducible from the entities' books and the ownership graph.

### 12.4 FX translation and remeasurement

Two distinct law kinds, never one rule set. **`Remeasurement`** (the temporal method, ASC 830-10-45-17/18 and 55-10; from the currency of record into the functional currency, used when an entity keeps records in a currency other than its functional currency and for highly inflationary economies under ASC 830-10-45-11, with an `IAS29` restatement variant in the IFRS registry): monetary items at the closing rate; non-monetary items and their related expense (depreciation, cost of sales, amortization) at the historical rates on the originating legs; other income-statement items at the registered average-rate document; the difference to P&L. **`Translation`** (the current-rate method, ASC 830-30 / IAS 21; from the functional currency into the reporting currency, performed only in a consolidation book): *all* assets and liabilities at the closing rate, equity at historical rates, income at the average rate, the difference to the registered CTA account in AOCI; CTA is recycled to P&L by the `Deconsolidate` rule on disposal. Both are delta-cumulative (each run posts only the change from the prior cumulative amount), and both record every rate document consumed, so the CTA roll-forward is explainable to the leg.

### 12.5 Comparatives and the originally-reported view

Every statement run may request comparatives (`FOR PERIOD FY2025 WITH COMPARATIVE FY2024`) and the restatement views (§7.4); a run `AS ORIGINALLY REPORTED` pins known-at to the first `StatementIssuance` of the period (§7.4), and `AS REPORTED IN <certificate>` pins the exact closure a filed statement used. A **`StatementIssuance { definition_oid, period, certificate_oid, issued_to: {Board, Auditor, Lender, Regulator, SEC(form), TaxAuthority, Other}, document_oid }`** is the journaled control object recorded by the `IssueStatements` intent when statements leave the entity; it is what "previously issued" means for ASC 250 and for every later diff. The restatement diff certificate (§8.3) is a statement run over both views with the delta per line and the contributing lineage.

### 12.6 Regulatory schedules and XBRL

Call-report and FR Y-9C schedules are `StatementDefinition`s whose lines map to the bank's chart with the regulatory instructions' version recorded; XBRL export maps definitions to taxonomy concepts (US GAAP / IFRS taxonomies by version) with contexts derived from the coordinate and units from the unit registry, validated against the taxonomy's calculation linkbase (foots by construction because the definition's subtotals are exact). The mapping is data; the export is certified; the filed instance's digest is recorded as a `Document` of kind `Disclosure` attested by the filer. FFIEC validity and quality edits are a versioned data registry run as `regulatory.edits(<form>@<quarter>)`; schedule lines that need instrument attributes the GL does not hold (RC-K, RC-N, RC-R) declare them as inputs from the `Instrument` graph, never from the GL alone.

---

## 13. Notary: Proofs, Bundles, Attestations, and Outsider Verification (Bet A3, part 2)

### 13.1 What is provable, and what is not

Provable to an outsider holding only a bundle and a trusted root: that an entry with digest *d* was recorded in the book's journal at position *i* (inclusion); that the journal at root *r₂* extends the journal at root *r₁* without rewriting (consistency); that a register cell's value is the fold of exactly the listed legs (recomputation, with the legs' inclusion proofs); that a statement's rows are the certified computation of the named definition over the named registers (replay). **Not** provable by these means: that the entry reflects what actually happened (that is what evidence, attestations, and professional judgment are for), that a filtered extract is complete without a range proof over the accumulator, or that a party did not keep a second set of books elsewhere. Proof scopes are labeled verbatim in every bundle (`inclusion`, `consistency`, `recomputation`, `replay`, `membership_only`), inheriting the substrate's discipline. **Completeness scope.** An accumulator proves inclusion and consistency of *sequence* ranges; it cannot prove that a filter by period or entity is complete unless the verifier sees every leaf in the range. Every extract and bundle therefore carries `completeness_scope ∈ {SeqRangeComplete(from, to) — every leaf digest in the range is listed and the verifier recomputes the root; ScopeIndexComplete — admissible only as defined below; FilterAttested — the producer attests the filter and the verifier checks membership only}`. Every TransparencyCheckpoint also commits scope_index_root over sorted `(book_id, branch_id, account_vid, effective_day, leaf_index)` tuples for its journal prefix, as a derived rebuildable index. `ScopeIndexComplete` is admissible only when the verifier derives every required index interval from authenticated scope inputs and proves each interval including empty intervals and boundaries. With `(book, branch, account_vid, effective_day, leaf_index)` ordering, an exact-account date interval is contiguous; a whole-book date interval or arbitrary account subtree is not generally one interval. Multi-range membership without authenticated complete account enumeration does not prove global coverage. For unsupported predicates, export supplies every leaf envelope in the selected authenticated checkpoint prefix and applies the predicate locally (`SeqRangeComplete`), or explicitly returns `FilterAttested/membership_only`. Predicate fields absent from LeafEnvelope, including entity dimensions, require the corresponding authenticated payload and metadata closure; payloads must match their record digests and referenced metadata must match the selected coordinate. Omitted or unauthorized predicate inputs preclude a completeness claim. The prefix boundary comes from the coordinate/checkpoint, never from the first and last matching records returned by the producer, and the bundle cannot claim a known-at frontier beyond that authenticated prefix without a verified extension. Do not discard period-scope support: the complete-prefix fallback is correct but has linear envelope cost. An additional period-major index is an optional measured optimization; it does not make arbitrary entity or subtree scopes contiguous. `O(k log n)` claims apply only to the named supported scope, witness construction, and trust assumptions, not to every bundle check.

`fa export ads --with-proofs` uses ScopeIndexComplete only for a supported authenticated scope; full-chart period scopes use the authenticated complete-prefix fallback unless an exact multi-range proof is available. Capability restrictions may require refusal or an explicit membership_only result rather than disclosing out-of-scope envelopes.

### 13.2 The journal accumulator and the bundle format

An authenticated index root is not by itself proof that the index was derived correctly from the journal. FA-INV-14's index-to-journal recomputation remains mandatory for a recomputation claim. A verifier either reconstructs from the selected journal prefix or extends previously verified state with a complete verified delta. Any distinct construction attestation and its trusted signer are labeled explicitly as a weaker premise; that attestation does not satisfy FA-INV-14's recomputation checker or authorize an unqualified independently recomputed ScopeIndexComplete result. A root/time witness signature does not attest correct indexing or ledger contents. A small membership proof cannot silently replace this derivation obligation. Negative vectors omit a whole account, omit an empty interval, change a hierarchy, insert a late-effective entry within the selected prefix, and authenticate a deliberately inconsistent index root. Golden negative fixtures for `fa-verify` also include one omitted in-scope leaf within a returned account, a retroactive entry recorded after the checkpoint, and a scope straddling two checkpoints. The after-checkpoint fixture rejects a claimed later known-at frontier without a verified extension; it does not claim completeness beyond the authenticated prefix.

The **journal accumulator** is a Merkle Mountain Range over canonical entry encodings (every `Entry` payload, control entry, attestation, and document receipt in commit order), maintained by Notary **in commit order as a derived, rebuildable structure** — never written inside the posting transaction, so there is no shared hot object on the write path — whose root at any `CommitSeq` is a pure function of the journal prefix (anyone can recompute it), with `AccumulatorState` objects journaled at a configured cadence so the root at a checkpoint is itself content-addressed and inclusion proofs for any entry are computed on demand from the maintained peaks. Leaf *i* is the domain-separated **leaf envelope** `BLAKE3("fa:acc-leaf:v1" ‖ canonical(LeafEnvelope { kind, book_id, branch_id, commit_seq, within_commit_ordinal, record_digest, class, effective_day, account_vids: sorted[], unit_ids: sorted[] }))` — so the envelope, not the checkpoint, binds each record's commit sequence and within-commit ordinal, leaf order is `(commit_seq, ordinal)`, the verifier checks monotonicity, a checkpoint's `leaf_count` fixes the sequence-to-prefix boundary that `AS KNOWN AT seq` selects (binary search over envelopes), and the selection fields let a verifier apply a scope predicate without payloads; `AccumulatorState` is a vertex whose `prev_state_oid` is written under a CAS guard; and every `TransparencyCheckpoint` additionally records the substrate's chain commitment at that sequence (available today as `NativeResultCertificate.snapshot_identity`), giving two independent chains now — the MMR cross-anchor (R-MMR) later replaces the commitment with an inclusion proof. It is deliberately at the *accounting* layer rather than only at the storage layer: an outsider's verifier must verify accounting facts without understanding the substrate's object formats, and the FrankenAccounting accumulator cross-anchors into the substrate's MMR when §5.8 lands (each `AccumulatorState` is then itself an object in the substrate's history, giving two independent chains). `TransparencyCheckpoint {book, commit_seq, root, signers[], timestamp_evidence}` objects are signed by the book owner and optionally by **witnesses** — the external auditor, a regulator, a trustee — under a registered `WitnessPolicy`, so a company cannot later present a different history to the same witness (detection) or to anyone, if the witness quorum rule is met (prevention), with the claims labeled exactly as the substrate labels them.

An **evidence bundle** (`.fab`, canonical, versioned) contains: the bundle manifest (book identity, coordinate, capability under which it was produced, redaction markings, producer principal and recorded time, scope label); the entries in scope with their payloads; their provenance closures (documents inline or by digest with size and kind when redacted or external); inclusion proofs against a named checkpoint; the checkpoint with its signatures; register cells and their fold proofs when balances are in scope; statement rows and certificates when reports are in scope; findings and their citations when procedures are in scope; and a human-readable `MANIFEST.md` generated from the manifest. Bundles are deterministic (same scope + same checkpoint ⇒ same bytes), so two productions of the same material are byte-identical and a court can compare them — the deterministic body excludes who produced it and when: those live in a detached `ProductionReceipt { bundle_digest, producer_principal, produced_at_seq, capability_commitment, time_evidence_oid, signature }` that signs the body's digest, while the scope, checkpoint, and redaction contract (which change content) stay inside the body.

A **`ProductionProfile`** governs productions (§0.4 decision 4): a Bates prefix and next number, journaled per matter (a document produced in two matters carries both ranges and `fa doc show` lists them); confidentiality designations under the protective order (`CONFIDENTIAL`, `ATTORNEYS' EYES ONLY`) per document; a **privilege log** generated from redaction contracts and withheld documents (date, author, recipients, description, basis); load-file export (DAT/OPT or EDRM XML) in `fa-formats`; and `fa exhibit mark <ref> --label "Px-12"`, which records deposition and trial exhibit labels as attestations. The FRE 902(13)/(14) package in a bundle includes the qualified person's statement template, the hash-verification procedure, and the written-notice text the rules require to be served on adverse parties. Signatures are `Ed25519` (RFC 8032) over domain-separated transcripts that exclude the signature fields; public trust anchors are a `TrustAnchorSet` document (signer keys with validity intervals) signed by the book's root key and rotated by `KeyRotation` control records; `fa-verify` therefore carries its own Ed25519 and BLAKE3 implementations with golden vectors inside its std-only budget.

### 13.3 `fa-verify`: the dependency-free verifier

**Balance proof closure.** A bundle that reports a stock balance includes the authenticated fold inputs for the in-scope stock cells from inception through the stock cutoff, or an explicitly justified starting state plus the complete intervening fold. Current-period flow entries alone cannot recompute a stock balance. An internal RegisterCheckpoint is a rebuildable cache, not proof of its value. If an opening balance is supplied only by an attestation, the report labels that premise and separately verifies the delta; it cannot describe the resulting stock amount as independently recomputed from inception. Existing inclusion, consistency, recomputation and attestation grades remain per predicate rather than one misleading bundle-wide success bit. No mandatory second index or new checkpoint signature regime is implied. Baselines must match scope, known-at, unit/valuation and accounting semantics; the delta is independently complete. A bundle with no stock claim needs no opening history. A stock claim without authenticated prior inputs or baseline is refused.

`fa-verify` is a separate crate with a registered dependency allowlist of exactly `std`: BLAKE3 (the substrate's implementation vendored by construction through `fgdb-crypto` is *not* linked — the verifier carries its own several-hundred-line BLAKE3 with golden vectors, so it is auditable in isolation), canonical decoders for the bundle schemas, MMR inclusion/consistency verification, signature verification for the registered schemes, exact-integer fold recomputation, and the statement replay for the definition kinds. Budget: several KLOC. It ships as a static binary and as a library, it is the differential oracle for the producer (§16.3), and its output is a verification report with every check's result and scope label. A regulator, an opposing expert, or a court-appointed neutral can run it on an air-gapped machine.

### 13.4 Legal and regulatory mapping (qualified)

FrankenAccounting states technical properties and lets counsel map them to law: the append-only journal with immutable recorded time and supersession lineage *is* a complete, time-stamped audit trail of every change (the shape SEC Rule 17a-4(f)'s 2022 audit-trail alternative describes); bundles carry canonical hashes and a certification template a qualified person can sign for FRE 902(13)/(14) — authentication only; the business-records foundation (FRE 803(6): made at or near the time by a person with knowledge, kept in the regular course of a regularly conducted activity) is *supported* by `recorded_at`, `Principal`, `TimeEvidence`, and custody receipts, and the FRE 902(11) certification template names those fields; certified statements are summaries of voluminous records whose underlying entries are producible (FRE 1006); procedure certificates name the method, parameters, and data so an expert's methodology is reproducible and testable (FRE 702); custody receipts and access logs support chain-of-custody testimony; retention floors and legal holds support document-preservation obligations; the transparency log with witnesses supports a showing that records were not altered after a trigger event (18 U.S.C. §1519 defenses are counsel's domain, the hashes are ours). None of this is a claim of admissibility or compliance; the plan's claim is that the technical predicates those rules name are present, enumerable, and testable.

### 13.5 Transparency checkpoints and witnesses

Checkpoint cadence is a policy (every close, every *n* commits, on demand before a production). A witness is a principal with the `witness` capability who signs checkpoints under the policy; the auditor's signature on the year-end checkpoint is the modern successor of the auditor's initials on the ledger page. Witness policies, quorum rules, and the exact detection-versus-prevention claims are inherited from the substrate's §5.8 vocabulary.

### 13.6 Replay certificates

Every report, finding, plan, and bundle carries a `Certificate {closure: {book root, branch head, known-at, effective selector, definition/procedure oid and version, parameters digest, registry epochs, rule versions, laws, rate and basis documents, capability}, output_digest, replay_command, completeness_grade}`. `fa replay <certificate>` reproduces the output and compares digests; every substrate read inside a certificate's closure is executed with `Database::execute_certified`, the FrankenAccounting `Certificate.inputs` carries each `NativeResultCertificate` (whose `snapshot_identity` is the chain commitment — this *is* the "book root" field), and replay calls `Database::replay` per input before recomputing the fold, so replay coverage is stated exactly: native reads and `fnx` procedure evidence, never writes or subscriptions; a retention policy or erasure that removed part of the closure downgrades the grade attributably (`Redacted`, `VerifiableIfArtifactsSupplied`) rather than failing silently. This is the expert-witness guarantee: a damages schedule computed in 2026 replays in 2029. `fa certificate status <oid>` returns `Current | SupersededBy(case_ref, later entries)` by checking for entries recorded after the certificate's known-at with effective dates inside its selector, so a 2026 schedule and a 2028 re-run after a restatement are never mistaken for each other.

---

## 14. Steward: Governance, Capabilities, and Segregation of Duties

### 14.1 Threat model

Registered in `threat_model.toml` with mitigations per threat: an insider with administrative access who wants to alter history (immutability and transparency checkpoints with external witnesses make alteration detectable; there is no "fix the row" path); collusion between preparer and approver (dual control for the dangerous classes; access logs; JET procedures on self-approval and timing); a compromised or confused agent (branch-per-task, dry-run defaults, amount ceilings and account scopes on its capability, promotion requires a human or a separately scoped approver agent); a stolen capability token (short TTLs, caveats, revocation journaled, every use logged); a rollback attack restoring an old book directory (the substrate's incarnation continuity and the witnessed checkpoints; the book's accumulator root is published to witnesses); a regulator or litigant who must read but must not see privileged material (redaction contracts with proofs; production profiles); a subpoenaed administrator who must prove non-alteration (checkpoint chain and witness signatures); erasure obligations in tension with retention (typed tombstones, attributable downgrade, legal holds).

### 14.2 Capability caveats

`capability_caveats.toml` registers the caveat vocabulary compiled into substrate predicates: `books ⊆ {…}`, `entities ⊆ {…}`, `accounts ⊆ subtree(code)`, `periods ⊆ {…}` or `effective ≤ date`, `dimensions ⊆ {…}`, `ops ⊆ {read, post, post_closed, approve, reconcile, close, reopen, restate, rule_change, issue_capability, witness, verify, produce, hold, erase}`, `classes ⊆ EntryClass`, `amount ≤ Amount` (per entry, per unit), `daily_total ≤ Amount`, `known_at ≤ seq` (an auditor pinned to fieldwork's snapshot), `branch = <draft>` (an agent confined to its draft), `redaction = <contract>`, `expires_at`, `requires_dry_run`. Caveats are conjunctive and attenuable only (a holder can narrow, never widen); issuance and revocation are journaled control entries. The `ops` vocabulary is closed and complete: `read, post, post_softclosed, post_closed, approve, reconcile, close, reopen, restate, migrate, schema_change, rule_change, issue_capability, witness, verify, produce, hold, erase, privileged, receivership, partial_view` — every capability name the admissibility matrix, the profiles, or any verb cites resolves to this list (a parity test enumerates them). **Authority is evaluated against current policy at commit, not at plan time**: the commit re-reads the `Capability` metadata vertex (revocation, expiry) and the book's `security_policy_epoch` as point-read witnesses, so a token revoked between dry run and commit yields `PlanInvalidated { CapabilityRevoked }`; historical selectors never resurrect authority — a token is valid or not *now*, whatever known-at the read selects; an approval's capability must be valid when the approval is recorded, and revocation afterwards does not unwind the recorded approval but is visible in its lineage. **Ceilings have one measure**: `amount ≤ A` bounds the mantissa of every leg in its unit; `daily_total ≤ A` bounds the sum of gross debit mantissas per unit per *root issuance* across every attenuated token derived from it (attenuation never resets a ceiling), over the book's calendar day under its `time_zone_policy` with the day boundary taken from `TimeEvidence` ticks, debited serially on the single writer through a per-issuance `UsageCell` reservation vertex updated by CAS in the posting transaction — the second registered, scoped exception to Appendix G's hot-object law, rebuildable from the journal and priced in Appendix G. **Scope and whole entries**: an entry is observable under a capability iff every leg's account and dimensions are in scope; otherwise the entry is invisible and a fold over in-scope accounts still includes its in-scope legs (a balance cannot exclude legs that exist), with `fa explain balance` listing only in-scope legs and marking the rest `partially_visible` only when the token carries `partial_view` — a partial fold is never described as whole-book, and the certificate names the capability commitment.

**Expiry admission.** The security policy defines the time source, freshness bound, rollback refusal, and admission/publication linearization point used for `expires_at`. Re-reading unchanged expiry metadata is insufficient: the clock-capable admission boundary obtains an admissible fresh time observation and passes it as an explicit input to pure `fa-policy` evaluation. Its source, uncertainty and acceptance point are retained as TimeEvidence or registered authorization evidence referring to it. Canon and Lantern still have no clock capability, and the Kernel does not call upward into Governance for policy evaluation. Any separately published authorization-time tick precedes acquisition of an aggregate plan's guarded basis; a later required tick invalidates that basis. Refuse if the permitted time evidence cannot decide validity at the selected admission point; a stale journal tick is not an extension of a token.
